So is a name the e2e battery imports from `dist` — typecheck cannot see those, which is how `reportVersionChange` got dropped and the telemetry-events spec died with "is not a function". Barrel re-exports stay too. `@reticlehq/core` has none of this class — every export is re-exported from the package barrel. Other packages are follow-ups.

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` — there is one `InstrumentationGapKind` vocabulary.** Honesty and `reticle_assert` spoke core (`no-signal-on-mutation`, `no-store-registered`). `reticle_domain` spoke a server-local copy (`missing-signal`, `unregistered-store`) of the same absences, so an agent that read both saw two names for one gap. Oracles now use the core kinds. `missing-testid` — the one kind only oracles emitted — is a core kind, so a later honesty emit cannot pick a third name.
- **`@reticlehq/server` — a numeric tool argument the code cannot honour is now refused, not silently dropped.** An unknown parameter is already refused with the strongest sentence on the surface: it was NOT applied, and a result computed without it would look like an answer. A _known_ parameter carrying a value the handler cannot honour is the same failure with better manners, and it used to pass. Measured live: `reticle_state { depth: -5 }` returned the full unscoped store, because `capDepth` treats a negative budget as "no cap". `reticle_network { limit: 1e9 }` was accepted against a ring buffer of 2000 events.

  Each bound is a policy, not a blanket rule. `since: 0` is a real cursor. `timeout_ms: 0` on assert means evaluate now. `depth: 0` collapses a node to a size marker. `threshold` is a 0–1 ratio, so `.int()` would refuse 0.01. A lock over every advertised tool input fails if a new `z.number()` ships with no floor, or (except cursors) no ceiling, so the next silent ignore cannot accumulate the way this one did.

### Fixed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle verify` ignored the port in `.reticle.json`, and its docstring said the opposite.** `parseCliArgs` already folds `RETICLE_PORT` and the project config into `defaultPort`, but the verify result had no `port` field, so `handleVerify` fell back to 4400 every time. A project on any other port got `MSG_NO_SESSION` ("your app is not running") rather than "I looked at the wrong port". `--port` was also rejected as an unknown argument.

  Verify now carries the resolved port, `--port` overrides it, and the docstring is finally true.

- **Repo — `pnpm vitest run` from the root no longer collects other agents' worktrees.** A root invocation used to walk `.claude/worktrees/**` (and the Cursor equivalent) — full checkouts of other branches — and report their failures as this checkout's. `pnpm test:unit` was never affected: it runs through turbo, scoped per package. The default root config now excludes those trees, so narrowing to one file from the root reports only this checkout.
- **`@reticlehq/server` — a full-document navigation no longer ends the drive.** `reticle_act_and_wait` on a link tore the SDK down with the old document and graded `unknown / observation_lost`; the following `reticle_assert` then refused the dead session id even though the new page had already connected. The only workaround was a fresh `reticle_lease` per URL, so on a server-rendered app a navigation itself was never verifiable.

  `reticle_navigate` already waited for the SDK to HELLO back and returned the new id. `reticle_act_and_wait` now does the same follow when the document is replaced mid-wait: it waits for the unique same-origin successor, evaluates the predicate there, and returns the new `sessionId`. `reticle_assert` (and every other tool) rebinds a stale id the same way, so the agent does not have to discover the successor itself. Two live tabs at that origin is still a guess and still refused; an id that was never connected still is.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an unread response body no longer downgrades a verdict that already held on a channel the body does not own.** `act_and_wait` declared a 201 and the exact unique message text; both passed; the verdict came back `unknown / outcome_unread` solely because bodies were not captured. The agent then went to enable capture instead of finishing the drive. The unread clause is right when the body is the only remaining channel — a 200 with batch or GraphQL errors inside — and it still fires there. When the caller named a string on screen, a store path, or a signal, and that held, the body is no longer the only channel. The unread write still rides out in `because`, the way an absence-derived contradiction still rides out in `contradictions`. A net-only declaration is unchanged: then the body is the remaining channel.
- **`@reticlehq/server`: `reticle_sessions` now declares the `attachment` history in its output schema.** The session manager attaches the tab's disconnect record to every row: how long it has been attached, how many times it dropped and reconnected, and how long the last drop lasted. That history decides whether a verdict over the window can be trusted. The tool's schema never declared the field, and a strict MCP client validates results against that schema, so on exactly the clients that validate, the outage history was silently stripped while the row still looked complete.

  Declared like every other field the handler returns; absent means the tab never dropped, so silence keeps meaning trustworthy.

- **An OAuth-style popup flow is no longer reported as `response-ignored`.** A click that opens another browsing context (a Google sign-in is the archetype) posts successfully and continues in a window the in-page SDK cannot follow, so the original tab legitimately never changes — and the contradiction layer read that as the client ignoring the response, an app defect, when the client had handed the consequence to a context nobody can see.

  The SDK now emits `context.opened` when the page calls `window.open`, and a successful write with no local movement inside a window where a context opened reports the new `consequence-elsewhere` finding instead: absence-derived, so it downgrades to unknown with the blind spot named rather than asserting failure about code that may be fine.

- **Docs + `@reticlehq/server` — the retired `RETICLE_TOOL_PROFILE` env is no longer advertised as the live tool-surface knob.** `reticle_tools` told agents to change it and restart; that mapping still exists so old shells do not break, but the switch that actually expands the surface is `RETICLE_ADVERTISE_ALL_TOOLS=1`. The `StartOptions.toolProfile` docs and the `verify` busy-port warning (it has printed three options for a while, not a raw `EADDRINUSE` stack) now match the code.
- **install gate — a connected session with nothing to observe no longer counts as a pass.** `pnpm gate:install` treated "a session connected" as success. An app can connect with `registerCapabilities({ testids: [], signals: [], stores: [] })`, so `hasCapabilities` stays false and `reticle_state` has nothing to read, permanently. That is the end state the gate exists to catch, and it was scored green. The gate now fails unless a connected session advertised capabilities; it stamps a probe `data-testid` into each scaffold so an empty create-vite app has something `init` can register, rather than failing every empty app for a reason that is true and uninformative. Callers that do not ask (the soak) keep the old connected-is-enough meaning.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a `reticle_assert` verdict reported a `file:line` that could point at completely unrelated code.** An assertion drives nothing, so the tool had no location of its own and used the one the PREVIOUS action remembered. Driven against a real Next.js app: an assert about the site navigation was journaled with the file and line of the button an earlier click had touched, and an assert that matched nothing on the page at all was journaled with that same line. The old value was, in both cases, a location this verdict had never looked at — it sent the agent to innocent code, and because it is persisted into the session journal and read back by `reticle_context`'s `proven`, the wrong pointer outlived the turn that produced it.

  A verdict now points only where it actually looked. An `element`/`text` assertion reports the matched element's own `file:line` — the descriptor already carried it — and reports nothing when the matched elements carry no location or disagree about it. The one borrow that remains is the documented one, on a failure only: a `signal`/`net`/`state` assertion has no element to point at, and the handler that should have fired the missing signal lives with the control last driven, which is what the tool's output schema has always said the field means. Everywhere else the field is now OMITTED, because an absent pointer costs the agent nothing and a wrong one costs it the trip.

  The journal record and the tool response are computed once and shared, so a verdict can no longer carry two different locations. `reticle_wait_for` gets the same rule; `reticle_act_and_wait` is unaffected, as it drove the element it names.

- **`@reticlehq/browser`: a drag now announces the crossing with boundary events, so drag-to-select extends.** A drag dispatched a held-button `mousedown`, a stepped path of moves and a clean `mouseup`, but never `mouseover`/`mouseout`/`pointerover`/`pointerout` or their non-bubbling enter/leave pairs as the pointer crossed from one element to another. React synthesises `onMouseEnter` from delegated `mouseover`/`mouseout`, so the standard drag-to-select grid (anchor set on `onMouseDown`, extended on `onMouseEnter`, cleared by a window-level `mouseup`) never grew its selection: every cell stayed exactly as the initial press left it.

  The crossing is now announced where it happens: out/leave on the source when the path first leaves its box, over/enter on the destination when it first arrives inside theirs, with `buttons` still held and `relatedTarget` naming the element on the other side of the boundary, matching what a real pointer emits. A free drag (no target) crosses no boundary and fires none.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a config discovered in another monorepo app no longer produces a contradictory `reticle init` action.** The no-session prose already treated a `.reticle.json` found outside the daemon's directory as positive evidence of a scope problem, but `next_action` did not receive that discovery and could simultaneously claim no config was found. Both halves now consume the same live scope facts, name the discovered directory and project ID, and recommend scoping the daemon to that app or acquiring its URL with `reticle_lease` instead of reinitializing a working install.
- **`@reticlehq/server`: a pooled lease no longer shares Chromium's throttling with its own background pages.** A pooled browser serves several lease contexts while only one page is ever the visible one, and for every other page Chromium throttles timers, suspends rAF and deprioritizes the renderer. That is exactly the state a session reports as `throttled: true`: timer- and WebSocket-driven DOM updates land seconds late, an assert times out on content that renders right after the timeout with no refetch, and the app gets blamed for being parked.

  Both launch sites (`playwright-launcher` for the pool, `real-input` for `reticle drive`) now pass Chromium's documented opt-outs (`--disable-background-timer-throttling`, `--disable-backgrounding-occluded-windows`, `--disable-renderer-backgrounding`) from one shared builder, so the two paths cannot drift apart. The switches change what Chromium is allowed to do to a page nobody is looking at; they do not change what Reticle reports about it, and whether a real pooled lease still reports `throttled: true` for some remaining reason is a question this fix raises but does not answer.

- **`@reticlehq/server`: the throttled-tab escape hatch is named in-protocol before it is named as a CLI command.** An agent whose only connected session was a hidden, throttled tab followed the advice to "run `reticle drive <url>`" and could not: an MCP-only client has no shell, so the recommendation sent it out of the protocol to do something only a human could do. The same sentence sat in all three places an agent meets while hunting a way out: the `reticle_sessions` description, the `recommendation` payload on each affected session row, and the recovery hint for throttled-tab refusals.

  All three now lead with the route the agent itself can take, `reticle_run { tool: "reticle_lease", action: "acquire", url }`, and keep `reticle drive <url>` in the sentence as the human-side equivalent. This finishes what the timeout recovery already did when it stopped naming bare `reticle_lease`: recovery advice the caller cannot act on is spent on discovery instead of the retry.

- **`@reticlehq/server`: scroll-to-find turns around, so a row above a bottomed virtualized list is reachable.** A list that was already scrolled to its end keeps its earlier rows mounted above the viewport, and the search only ever walked downward: the first step reported "at end" and the answer came back `found:false / exhausted:true` for a row that was one page up. The bisection path had the same shape, since its refinement also assumed down, so an overshooting jump was unrecoverable too.

  The walk now continues upward when the bottom is reached, and after a bisection jump it refines from wherever the jump landed in whichever direction the list still has. `exhausted` means what it should have meant all along: both directions are spent, not "the bottom is here". Each direction gets the caller budget in full, so a call that walks to one end and back can cost up to twice `maxScrolls` steps, and the answer is only `exhausted` when both ends are actually seen.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `write-field-ignored` no longer fires on a command bus whose response is a snapshot, not an echo.** A write that returns OK and echoes a different value than it was asked to set is a real dropped field, and that is still caught. What was not a dropped field: a `POST` of `{command:"chat.send", ...}` answered with the current viewer snapshot. The message arrived over the socket and rendered; Reticle treated a snapshot field that happened to share a name with the request as an attempted echo, and graded a healthy send as `write-field-ignored`.

  The response now has to look like a restatement of the request before any key is compared. A discriminator the request carries (`command`, `action`, `op`, `procedure`) that the response does not repeat is not an echo. Neither is a fat body that shares one coincidental key. A preferences write that echoes `locale` nested under `saved` still fires; a command-shaped write whose response restates the command still fires. The existing single-value and not-echoed-at-all guards are unchanged — this narrows on shape, not on value types.

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` — a wait that times out on a throttled tab no longer looks like a missing render.** A hidden tab is starved by the browser: after a reload it sits on "Loading" forever, `reticle_wait_for { kind: "text", value: "Configuration" }` times out as a near-miss, and that sentence is identical to "the code did not render". The agent then "fixes" working code. The session already carried `throttled: true` on the health envelope; agents treat the near-miss prose as the verdict.

  When the session is throttled, a failing wait or assert now sets `inconclusive` and prefixes the failure with that fact — so `reticle_assert` grades UNKNOWN rather than assertion-failed. The structured near-miss (`observed` / `expected` / `assertion`) is kept beside it. A pass is unchanged. The in-protocol escape hatch is named on that note; the `reticle drive` recommendation copy is a separate change.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — Windows `init` now prints the `cmd /c` MCP fallback even when `claude mcp add` succeeded.** The registered command is still bare `npx` on every platform. The `cmd /c` spawn is the one that PowerShell's execution policy does not gate, and it used to be printed only when the `claude` CLI was missing. The reported Windows install had `claude mcp add` succeed, zero `reticle_*` tools, and a daemon that never started — so the one paragraph that would have unblocked it never appeared.

  `init` now prints that paragraph on every Windows run, as a NOTICE next to the registration, not as extra work. The majority-platform launch command is unchanged: defaulting it to `cmd /c` is still the riskier change, and this is the half that makes the fallback visible on the path that actually failed. The same text already lives in the no-agent manual step, and is not duplicated there.

- **`@reticlehq/server`: `write-field-ignored` no longer fires when the response is not an echo at all.** A command bus POSTs `{"command":"chat.send", ...}` and deliberately answers with the current viewer snapshot; the message itself arrives over WebSocket and renders. Collecting scalar values by key name at any depth made one key the two bodies happened to share read as an attempted echo of what was asked, so a write that fully applied was reported as half-applied.

  The diff now runs only when the response is echo-shaped: a strict majority of the request's scalar keys must reappear somewhere in it. An envelope restating most of what was sent still grades exactly as before; a snapshot that shares an incidental key or two does not. Like every guard in this file, that trades the rare genuinely half-echoed write away rather than cry wolf.

- **`@reticlehq/server`: a predicate failure waited out on a throttled tab now says the tab was throttled.** A starved tab and a missing element read identically as a bare near-miss: after a hard reload a backgrounded tab can sit on its loading state forever (hydration starved, nothing painted), and `reticle_wait_for { text }` timing out there looks exactly like "the code did not render" — the one answer an agent cannot tell apart from "the tab was never allowed to run". The health envelope already rode alongside every result saying `throttled:true`, but nothing connected it to the verdict an agent actually gates on.

  `reticle_assert` and `reticle_wait_for` now append the starvation fact to a failing verdict's own `failureReason`, with the remedy in the same sentence. The original diagnosis still leads, a pass is returned untouched, and on a healthy tab the failure says exactly what it always said.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an instrumentation gap told the agent an app was unverifiable and gave it nowhere to go.** A gap exists to be acted on: it names an absence in the app, the cost that absence imposed on the verdict just returned, and the one change that closes it. What it never carried was a location. The type declared an optional `file:line` and no producer had ever set one, so every gap went out with a `ref` and nothing else.

  A `ref` is a handle to a DOM node, and gaps are read late. `reticle_verify { action: "coverage" }` is the "am I done?" call, made long after the verdict that recorded the gap, by which time the page has re-rendered and the handle very likely resolves to nothing. So the surface aimed squarely at a build-and-verify loop was handing that loop a remedy with no address.

  A gap about a control that changed the DOM and signalled nothing now carries the `file:line` of that control, taken from what the build plugin already stamped and the act path already had in hand. It survives into the coverage answer intact, which is the point: `source` is a fact about the code and stays true while the `ref` beside it rots.

  **The other gaps stay unlocated, deliberately.** A store is registered once at app setup and a router adapter is wired app-wide; neither lives at the control that was driven, and lending them its line would send the agent to a file that cannot hold the fix — worse than silence, because it costs the trip as well. An absent pointer here means Reticle does not know, and it is not filled in with a guess.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the predicate grammar was reachable from nothing, and an agent that cannot find a grammar writes a weaker check.** A predicate parameter is advertised compactly on purpose: the union is recursive, and inlining it costs thousands of characters per parameter, re-sent every turn, to describe a grammar most calls use one variant of. The compact form carries the kind list and points at `reticle_tools` for the fields — but `reticle_tools` answered with the parameter's own prose, which for `reticle_act_and_wait { until }` reads "same shape as `reticle_assert`". So the one pointer the surface offered led to another pointer, and the fields of a kind could be learned only by guessing at them one rejected call at a time.

  **The cost that matters is not the round trips.** An agent that could not find `route`'s fields fell back to a `text` check where a route check was meant — a presence assertion standing in for a navigation assertion, which is precisely the false green this product exists to prevent. A grammar that is hard to discover pushes the caller toward the weakest oracle on the surface.

  `reticle_tools { names: [...] }` now returns the real field grammar of every kind whenever a named tool takes a predicate, derived from the schema so the two cannot drift. It rides on that reply only: the catalog listing and the advertised tool schemas are byte-identical to before, so no turn pays for a grammar it did not ask for. Two spellings that were costing calls now simply work — `type` read as the discriminator when `kind` is absent, and `of` read as `not`'s child the way it already was for `allOf`/`anyOf` — while a `kind` and a `type` that disagree are refused rather than silently resolved.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle_tools` answered a question nobody asked instead of refusing.** Every tool on the surface refuses a parameter it does not declare, because a dropped key means the reply is a well-formed answer to a different question and the caller cannot tell the two apart. The two meta-tools were exempt by accident: the allowed-key map was built from the tool table, and they are composed onto the advertised surface rather than living in it, so they had no entry — and no entry meant every key allowed. A call that misnamed the one parameter `reticle_tools` declares had the key dropped and got the entire catalogue back, on the one tool whose whole job is telling an agent what the surface is.

  The map now covers the advertised surface as well as the table, so the meta-tools are as strict as everything else. Any tool's refusal also now names what it **does** declare alongside what it does not — the list was already in hand, and for a tool carrying no example it is the difference between a typo and a second guess.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` + `@reticlehq/server` — a page read that stopped early can no longer be reported as a page with nothing in it.** The snapshot walk stops at its node cap and returns a document-order prefix with `truncated: true`. That flag has always been honest and has never been actionable: it says the read stopped, not _where_, so nothing could finish it. A caller received half a page and drew a conclusion from it — and the conclusions that break this way are the negative ones. "No error is shown", "the row is gone", "nothing on screen disagrees" are claims about the whole page, and half a page cannot support one. The record that would have contradicted them may simply have been past the cut.

  The walk now names its own frontier: the refs of the subtrees it never entered. A cut is therefore recoverable — re-read each branch at its own path and the union is the whole tree — and the completion loop that does this is bounded on both axes, in reads and in rounds, because an unbounded recursion over a hostile DOM is its own defect. When the frontier itself overflows, the read says the tree **cannot** be assembled rather than shortening the list and looking finished.

  **`reticle_reconcile` refuses instead of returning an empty comparison over a page it could not finish**, and names what stopped it. The asymmetry is deliberate and the whole point: a mismatch found in a partial page is still found — one witness proves presence — while an empty result is a universal claim. So the positive keeps working and only the negative is refused. Absence of evidence is not reportable as evidence of absence.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a verdict could cite evidence from a page that no longer exists.** An observation window was scoped by time and by buffer capacity and by nothing else, so it could still hold the network calls, console errors and signals of a document a full navigation or a reload had already thrown away — and any of them could be named as the cause of an action taken now. Reported from the field with a cited failing request that named a database row which had since been deleted: true about the bytes, false about the world, which is the worst kind of wrong a verification tool can be.

  Every observation now carries the document it belongs to, the session tracks which document is on screen by watching that same stream, and the contradiction pass drops anything belonging to a document that has been replaced. The scoping happens once, where events become evidence, rather than in each rule that reads them — reasoning about a dead page's traffic was a defect in all of them, not in whichever one reported it.

  **An absence is not an all-clear.** Where excluding that evidence leaves nothing behind, the answer is not "nothing happened" — that trades a wrong citation for a wrong all-clear, and the all-clear is the more expensive of the two. The window reports `evidence-superseded` instead, which downgrades the verdict to `unknown` rather than asserting a failure and names the remedy: the page was replaced, so drive the action again against the one now on screen. Telling that apart from a genuinely empty window is the whole point — an agent given the first knows to re-drive, and an agent given the second does not.

  An SPA route change is deliberately not a new document: same JavaScript context, same in-flight requests, same evidence. An SDK too old to report a document is unaffected in either direction, since evidence nobody could scope is still counted as current — a verification tool that quietly stops finding things is worse than one that occasionally over-reports.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an assertion could be answered by a page that no longer exists.** Scoping evidence to the document it was observed under stopped at the contradiction pass; the predicate engine kept its own event windows, and that is the sharper of the two — `reticle_assert` and `reticle_act_and_wait`'s `until` both resolve there, and both hand back a verdict an agent gates on. A network call, console entry, signal or animation recorded under a document that a full navigation or a reload has since thrown away could therefore decide an assertion about the page now on screen.

  Both directions were wrong, and both are now closed. A stale event that SATISFIED the assertion was a false green, the worst answer a verification tool can give; one that REFUTED it was a false red, which sends an agent to fix code that was never at fault. The window is filtered once, where every oracle takes it from, so no individual rule has to remember to do it.

  **A window emptied by that filter reports as inconclusive, never as false.** "It did not happen" is available and wrong there, and it downgrades the verdict to `unknown` instead — the answer names the page replacement and the remedy, which is to drive the action again against the document now on screen. `route` is deliberately untouched by the emptying: it can still answer from where the app is at this moment, and a reload is exactly the case that fallback exists for. Evidence from an SDK too old to report a document still counts as current, so nothing stops being checked.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a cloud call that stalls no longer hangs the tool call waiting on it.** Node's `fetch` has no default timeout: a connection that opens and then goes quiet never settles, and every cloud request in the server was awaited without one. The worst of them sat inside `reticle_flow_verify` on the `verify: 'server'` path, where a stalled hosted runner meant an MCP call that simply never returned — the failure mode this product treats as its worst, because an agent waiting on a tool has no way to notice, retry, or report. The same absence made every `reticle cloud …` subcommand able to sit at a blank terminal indefinitely.

  Every cloud request now goes through one client with a bounded budget. It is one budget, not eight, because they are all small JSON exchanges against the same API — with the single exception of the hosted verification submit, which legitimately blocks while a real browser runs the suite and so carries its own longer, still-finite budget rather than dragging the common one up to fit.

  **A timeout reads as a timeout.** A bare `AbortError` reaching a user or an agent is a riddle, so the abort is turned into a sentence that names the request, says the server accepted the connection and never answered, and points at the environment variable to check — ending with the fact that matters most to somebody blocked: verification works locally without cloud. Where the cloud was already best-effort, a timeout still degrades to local instead of failing the work.

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` + `@reticlehq/browser` — one name for one storage area.** Three files described the same three areas in three different vocabularies: core defined the enum and fed it to the `STORAGE_CHANGED` payload, the storage tool re-declared the same values as a fresh literal list, and the browser observer compared against a bare string two lines after using the enum correctly for its siblings. They did not agree, and the one that disagreed was the enum every other file was supposed to be reading from.

  Nothing had broken yet, because the cookie member had no consumers at all — which is precisely what let it drift for free. The moment anything read it, Reticle would have minted an area its own tool refused, and an agent taking the value off the wire contract and handing it back to `reticle_storage` is the ordinary way to use these tools. Being told your input is invalid, by the system that just produced it, is not a failure an agent can diagnose.

  The tool now builds its accepted areas from the contract instead of restating them, and the observer matches by the enum. The published spelling does not change: the outlier was the one with no callers.

- **`@reticlehq/server`: `duplicate-request` no longer reports every distinct write to one URL as a double submit.** A command-bus API posts every mutation to a single endpoint and discriminates on a JSON body field (`{"command":"study.stage.set"}` vs `{"command":"mesh.plan"}`). The rule keyed repeats on method + URL alone, so one click firing three genuinely different commands read as "the same write fired 3 times": nearly every `act_and_wait` in such a session was flagged, and verdicts that had genuinely passed degraded to `unknown` behind doubles that were never doubles. Reported from the field.

  A captured request body now joins the identity, so writes that differ in what they sent are no longer counted as repeats, and identical bodies still are. With body capture off (or a non-text body, which reports a type marker instead) there is nothing to compare and the URL alone stands, exactly as before; a window where only some calls carried bodies compares nothing rather than guess. The finding's `detail` still names `method url ×N`, so nothing downstream had to change.

### Removed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle demo` is gone, and nothing replaces it.** It existed to give a new user something impressive to watch, and the way it did that was the problem: a scripted tour of an app we shipped, driven by a command, proving nothing about the user's own codebase. Somebody who ran it saw Reticle work and still had no instrumented app, which is the exact gap they installed us to close.

  The moment it was built for is better served by the thing that was already there. The agent installs Reticle into the real project, drives one real flow of the real app, and reports the verdict — a headful browser, the user's own screen, their own bug. That is not a smaller version of the demo; it is the only version that is evidence. `init`'s closing hint now says so, and asks for it in the agent's words rather than as a command to copy.

  Removing a command is a breaking change for anyone who scripted it. Nothing else did — it took no arguments, produced no artifact, and was never part of the tool surface.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — dead HUD artwork no longer ships in every user's page bundle.** The presenter still uses the mark and the FAB. A ~5.5KB wordmark SVG, its unused HTML wrappers, and a handful of aliases (`DockAlign`, a stale presenter UI version constant, `PAUSED_BADGE_LABEL`, `CONTROLS_HEAD_HTML`, `isDockDragged`) had no importers. The HUD still mounts.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — two devDependencies nobody imported, one of them a native binary.** `sharp` had zero references anywhere in the package, and it is not in the root `onlyBuiltDependencies`, so its native build was skipped and the only thing it ever did was download a set of platform binaries on every clone and every CI job. `@types/jsdom` was the same kind of nothing: jsdom is consumed as the vitest _environment_, which needs no type package at all — `@reticlehq/react` has run the identical environment without it the whole time.

  **The `sharp` security override in the root manifest stays.** It looks orphaned once the direct dependency goes and it is not: `next` and `astro` both pull `sharp` as an optional dependency in the fixtures under `apps/`, so the override is still the floor on a transitive install. Removing a version floor because the direct dependant left is how a patched transitive quietly slides back down.

## [2.11.0] — 2026-08-22

### Added

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` — an action now reports what the APP did not tell Reticle, and the one change that would fix it.** Reticle has always answered "could I see it?" honestly: an unobservable channel downgrades a verdict rather than passing it. It has never answered the other half — _what would I need in order to see it?_ `reticle_act_and_wait` and `reticle_assert` now carry `instrumentationGaps`, each entry `{ kind, missing, cost, fix, source?, ref? }`: a control driven with no source mapping, a state assertion against an app that registers no store, a DOM change no signal announced, a route change nothing signalled.

  **Reported only where an absence made THAT verdict weaker**, never as a survey of the page. Each kind carries its own gate, because "weaker" means something different per kind: a missing source mapping costs nothing on a pass and a great deal on a red, where the file and line are the first thing wanted; a missing store costs nothing unless the caller asked about state; a silent mutation costs nothing if the app proved the outcome another way. A gap nobody hit is a backlog, and a backlog reported as a finding is how an agent learns to stop reading findings.

  The remedy is derived from the kind in one place rather than written at each emit site — a kind reported with a fix that has drifted from it is worse than no fix, because an agent that follows a wrong instruction spends the round trip _and_ ends further away.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an open gap means verification is not finished, and says so where the agent asks.** `reticle_verify { action: "coverage" }` already answered "am I done?" with the controls you did not drive. It now also answers with what the app **cannot answer**, which no amount of further driving will change, and adds `unproven: true` when that is why you are not finished. Reporting the first without the second is how an agent completes a pass believing it verified something the app was never able to confirm.

  The ledger keys on the LAST verdict, not the session's history: an agent that hits a gap, instruments the app and re-verifies has closed it, and a mechanism that still reported it would punish exactly the behaviour this exists to cause.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — of the controls you drove, how many Reticle could actually observe.** A new `observability` block on the coverage answer, and the first honest denominator the product has had. No percentage is reported for an empty run, because 0/0 rendered as 100% is the most flattering possible reading of no evidence at all. An app-level gap is never divided into a control, since an unregistered store is missing from the application and not from a button.

  **The floor ships with the number.** The best a project has reached is recorded in `project.json` and can only ever rise; a regression is reported when a later run falls below it. A coverage figure with no floor is one that gets gamed, and the cheapest way to stop a gap firing is to stop asserting the thing that revealed it.

### Fixed

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` — a saved flow lands in the project it verifies, and says where.** Everything Reticle persists — flows, the capability contract, baselines, capsules, cross-run memory — resolved against the daemon's own `process.cwd()`. That is a statement about where the daemon was launched, and it was being read as a statement about which project is being verified. Those are usually not the same tree: a user-scoped MCP registration is started wherever the editor likes. Reported three ways from the field — `ENOENT: mkdir '/.reticle'` from a daemon at `/`, a flow written into an unrelated repo, and a save that reported success without naming a path so it had to be found with `find`.

  Resolution is per call, from the session's own project, with **no wire change**: HELLO already stamps `projectId` and `.reticle.json` already declares the same id beside the code it configures. `reticle init` now also records where each project lives, so a daemon started in a different repo entirely can still resolve one. The read half and the write half go through one resolution — a `flow_save` that found the project while `verify_change` still read the daemon's directory would have been worse than the original defect, because the flow would save and then be invisible to the tool that exists to replay it.

  It refuses rather than guesses when two checkouts declare one project. A wrong root writes a caller's evidence into a tree they never drove.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the escape hatch is no longer offered by the output that just diagnosed why it is shut.** `reticle_lease` is what `status`, `doctor` and the no-session diagnosis all recommend when no tab is connected, and it drives a Reticle-owned Chromium — so a missing or wrong-revision browser is exactly the case where that advice cannot help. It is now qualified, with the pinned install command, and says plainly that a large download is the human's call rather than something an agent should run unprompted on their machine.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a `{net}` assertion over a request Reticle cannot observe is inconclusive, not failed.** The network observer instruments `fetch` and `XMLHttpRequest`, so a document-initiated load — a favicon, a manifest, a stylesheet, a font — was graded `assertion_failed`. That is a false red from the layer whose job is not to produce one, and worse than an unknown, because an agent that trusts it goes and "fixes" working code. Thanks to **@vaibhav8a**, who also expanded nested predicate fields in a parse error so `element.query` stops being a second round trip.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `sizeCost` counted UTF-16 code units under a field named `bytes`.**

### Changed

- **Repo — an issue we have already fixed can no longer keep reading as available work.** A commit that says `Closes #N` is a claim the work is done; if that issue is still open and unlabelled afterwards, the tracker is lying to the next person who reads it — and that person is looking for something to start. `pnpm check:stale-issues` refuses the branch unless the issue is closed or labelled `fixed-pending-release`, and ships with a self-test, because a guard nobody has watched go red proves nothing when it is green.

  This is here because it cost real people real evenings. **@Osheun** opened a fix for a dev-server probe defect and we shipped our own the next day without merging theirs — they were first. They also rebuilt config discovery that had shipped a day and a half earlier while its issue stayed open. **@hardikguptaofficialgit** rebuilt the `inViewport` predicate for the same reason, and in doing so found a real false green in the shipped version: it intersects only against the window, so an element scrolled out of an `overflow:auto` panel still reports as in the viewport. **@shaurya703** and **@vaibhav8a** independently solved the same two issues within half an hour of each other. Every one of those was our process, not their work.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — four guards that could pass over an empty collection now have a control each.** Thanks to **@shaurya703**, with the same fix independently written by **@vaibhav8a**. Thanks to **@slegarraga** for proving the MCP no-example fallback actually fires, rather than asserting around it.

### Added

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a licensed event says which organisation it belongs to, so PostHog can aggregate a customer instead of a machine.** Attribution already carried `licenseId`, but as a flat property, which means every per-customer question is a filter typed by hand against a UUID and there is no such thing as a company in the data. Events now also carry `$groups: { organization: <licenseId> }`, which is the shape PostHog aggregates natively: per-company retention, one row per customer rather than one per machine, and a view of which organisations have gone quiet.

  The group key is the licence id and nothing else. **A customer's machine still never transmits their name**, so an event carries no identity and the analytics side holds no customer list unless we deliberately upload one from the issuance ledger, which is the only place that mapping exists. Absent entirely on an unlicensed build: an empty group key would mint a phantom bucket that every OSS install falls into, and it would be the largest group on the dashboard.

### Changed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle license` stopped naming gated features to customers whose licence already works.** `gated` exists to close a dead end for somebody who has NOT bought: being told to set an environment variable with no idea what it unlocks. An `active` licence is the one reader that question is already answered for, so listing feature names at them revealed the product surface and answered nothing they asked. Every unlicensed status still reports it, and so does the one active case that cannot be explained without it, a key covering nothing the running build gates. `contact` remains on every status, because a stuck reader always needs somebody to write to.

### Fixed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the telemetry client ignored its own injected environment when resolving a licence.** `createTelemetry` takes an `env` for injection, and licence resolution read the ambient `process.env` instead. Identical in production, which is why it went unnoticed, and wrong everywhere the seam is actually used: an embedder or a test that injects an environment got attribution from the process it happened to be running in rather than the one it asked for. Found while asserting the group payload against the file sink, where the two environments differ for the first time.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the enterprise gate refused every valid licence on a real release.** Two entry points resolved the issuer key differently: `assertEnterpriseFromEnv` consulted the key baked at publish, while `assertEnterprise(ctx)` read only the environment. On a published build, where the key is baked and no env var is set, the same licence on the same build was ALLOWED by one and DENIED by the other with `no-issuer-key`. The only gated feature calls the ctx path, so it refused every customer, and whatever real feature got gated next would have been dead on arrival for the same reason. Both paths now resolve identically, baked first and the environment only as the dev and test hatch. Caught by driving the packaged, stamped artifact rather than the source tree, which is the only place the two can disagree.

### Removed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the `instrumentation_stalled` event.** Its founding argument was that a missing `app_instrumented` is an uninterpretable absence, unable to separate "never wired" from "the process died before it could say so". The event did not solve that: it emitted only on a flush tick or a graceful shutdown, so a killed daemon still sent `daemon_started` and nothing else and landed in the absence anyway. Everything it carried is derivable from `daemon_started` minus `app_instrumented`, joined on `sessionId`, and that set difference is strictly MORE complete because it does catch the killed daemons. A second way to compute the same number that disagrees with the first is worse than not having it.

  Two defects go with it, recorded so they are not rediscovered as new. The shutdown path had no floor, so any duration qualified and a run cancelled after three seconds in the wrong directory emitted a "stall"; any historical count read as a defect count is inflated by an unknown amount. And it had no causal resolution at all: "dev server never started", "wrong port", "SDK not injected" and "stale build" were one bucket.

  What was NOT removed is the CLOCK, which two live features read and neither is a metric: the no-session diagnosis tells an agent how long this daemon has waited, and the daemon still warns on stderr once a run passes ten minutes with nothing connected. It moved to `session/stall-clock.ts`, beside the files that read it, which is where its old home under `telemetry/` had disguised it as part of the event.

## [2.10.0] — 2026-08-21

### Added

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a service can now build ON this engine instead of forking it.** Everything below is additive and inert by default: nothing here changes a single verdict, tool answer or byte on the wire for anyone using Reticle normally. It exists because the alternative was a private fork of the engine, and a fork is how two copies of this engine start drifting on the rules that decide whether an app is broken — which is the one thing that must never differ between them. Four seams, each the smallest thing that removes a reason to edit this package: `ToolDeps.ext` carries a consumer's own dependencies through to its own tool handlers, typed rather than cast; `registerContradictionFold` adds a rule to the contradiction pass, so a consumer's oracles run in every verdict path this engine has rather than only in the calls it happens to make itself; `createMcpServer` and `advertisedTools` take the tool table instead of reading a fixed one, so a consumer serves its own tools beside ours on one surface — the same surface an agent already knows, which is the point. A registered rule that throws is contained and logged rather than propagated: it is somebody else's code inside `assert`, `act_and_wait`, `observe` and `crawl`, and a throw that escaped would turn one missing finding into a dead engine.

- **`@reticlehq/core` — a finding can now state how much authority it carries.** `FindingTier` and `tierOfFinding` name a distinction the verdict rule already turns on: an OBSERVED contradiction is positive evidence against the action and answers `no`, while an ABSENCE_DERIVED one was inferred from something not having happened yet, in a window Reticle chose the end of, and downgrades to `unknown`. Until now that was only ever computed at the point of use, so findings all arrived looking alike and an agent handed three of them could not tell which one decided the verdict and which was a note about when Reticle stopped looking. Additive and not yet consulted by this package's own verdict rule — rewiring that changes which findings downgrade, which is a product decision and gets its own change. A kind this vocabulary does not know is OBSERVED, deliberately: rules registered through the new contradiction seam emit kinds that are absent on purpose, and an unknown kind has made no claim about a chosen window, so downgrading it would invent a caveat on its author's behalf.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `findContradictions` and `createMcpServer` are exported.** Neither was in the package barrel, so anything embedding this engine had to re-implement the contradiction fold — a second implementation of the one thing this product is judged on, and the copy nobody dogfoods is the copy that rots. Found by compiling a consumer against the BUILT package rather than the source tree, which is the only place it is visible: everything inside this repo reaches them by relative path and never notices they are unreachable from outside.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an agent can report that something WORKED, not only that it broke.** Every kind `reticle_feedback` offered an agent was a complaint: bug, gap, ambiguity, feature_request, improvement. So the only thing the corpus could ever become was a defect list, and the question a refactor actually needs answered, which parts are worth protecting when we change them, had no evidence behind it at all. The `experience` kind and its 1-5 `rating` already existed in the contract and were reachable only by a human at a terminal; the agent, which is the user this product is built for, could not file one. Both are now on the agent tool and on `reticle feedback --agent`, and the MCP instructions name it so an agent that was never told about it can still use it.

  The rating is deliberately the smaller half. A model asked for a score will produce an agreeable one, and an agreeable score is indistinguishable from an earned one once both are in the same column, so the tool asks for it only when the judgement was formed while working and never on request, and says outright that the text naming the concrete moment is the part that can be acted on or quoted. Off-scale values are dropped at the handler as well as rejected by the schema: a number that reaches the wire out of range still averages, which is worse than an absent one.

### Changed

- **`@reticlehq/core` + `@reticlehq/server` — "I could not see" stopped meaning "nothing was wrong".** Two facts of opposite kinds shared one answer. By the time a verdict reaches the vacuous-grade clause, a failure, a contradiction, a dirty capture, a pending write and an unread one have all been ruled out — so when the window ALSO settled, the engine did not fail to see anything: it saw the whole window, ran every oracle and found nothing wrong. Reporting that as `unknown`, in the same word as a look that never finished, sent people to fix the wrong thing — one case needs "assert something", the other needs "look again with better coverage". `verified` gains a fourth value, `no-fault`, with both of its honesty properties enforced rather than documented: it requires a SETTLED window so it cannot be earned by returning early, and it is never `yes` so it cannot be mistaken for proof. `vacuous_grade` keeps its name and narrows to what it now means — nothing proved AND the window never settled. **If you gate on this field, note that `no-fault` is not a pass**; the agent-facing guidance in SKILL.md and the MCP instructions says so too, because an agent meeting an undocumented value on the field it gates on will read it as green.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `Contradiction.kind` and `CrawlAnomaly.kind` are typed `string`.** They were closed unions of this package's own vocabulary. A rule registered by a consumer emits kinds this package has never heard of, and a shared enum is exactly how somebody's private vocabulary ends up shipped in the free product by accident. Source-compatible for every reader — a string is what they already received at runtime — and this package's own emit sites stay checked against `ContradictionKind`, so a typo'd literal is still a compile error rather than a finding nobody can explain.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a licensed event stopped carrying the plan, which the ledger already holds.** Enterprise attribution shipped three properties on every event; one of them was redundant on the day it was written. `licenseId` is the join key and `licenseStatus` is the only thing that tells a lapse apart from a churn, but the PLAN is keyed by that same id in the issuance ledger, so sending it meant paying a per-event cost, from every machine, forever, to carry what one local file already knew, and the join that turns an id into a company turns it into a plan at the same moment. Cut before a release baked it into what customers send, since a property is easy to add and awkward to withdraw once it is in somebody's egress review. `reticle license` still reports it locally, where it costs nothing and answers a question the operator is actually asking.

### Fixed

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the stale-response rule fired on correct code, and could not fire on real traffic.** Two defects in one rule, pointing opposite ways. It reported a race whenever two reads of an endpoint settled out of order and never asked whether the app ACTED on the loser — but an app that guards against superseded responses races exactly as visibly and is not broken, so the rule accused correct code, and a detector that does that teaches people to ignore it and takes the true positives down with it. Adding the question exposed the second defect: a reaction had to land strictly AFTER the completion stamp, and browsers do not work that way. `NET_REQUEST` is emitted when the observer's wrapper sees the fetch settle and the app's own handler runs on that same microtask, so the state write, the signal and the DOM text routinely carry the EARLIER timestamp — meaning a strictly-forward window could never have fired on real traffic however plainly an app misbehaved. Sixteen unit tests passed over both, because every fixture placed the reaction after the cause, which is the shape a person writing a test reaches for and not the shape a recording has.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a working button was called dead on one silent sample.** "Your button does nothing" is the strongest claim the crawler makes and the one a reader acts on immediately, and it rested on a single click. Measured on a fixture whose FIXED twin has a working primary CTA, the crawl reported that CTA dead on 2 of 3 runs — always a control the drive had already clicked successfully moments earlier. A dead control is now confirmed with a second click, in its own window, because reusing the first would let the first click's absent activity decide the second. Anything that goes wrong during the confirmation reports "not dead": a confirmation that cannot run is not evidence, and a missed finding is the cheaper direction to be wrong in. Costs one extra round trip on the rare control that looked dead, and nothing on every control that did not.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an app that retracted its own claim was accused of asserting it.** Two rules read the same evidence and only one consulted whether the app had OWNED the failure. So an app that announced `ack:requested` optimistically, met a 500, then correctly emitted `ack:failed` and rolled the row back was reported as having explicitly asserted success — the strongest accusation the contradiction pass makes, levelled at code doing exactly the right thing. Ordering cannot decide this, which is why the fix is not "the claim must follow the failure": an optimistic UI legitimately fires its success signal BEFORE the response, so that rule would miss the real defect while still clearing the correct app. What separates them is not when the app spoke, it is whether it took it back.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — importing the library dragged in the installer.** `mcp/mcp.ts` and `mcp/proxy-handshake.ts` read the MCP server's own NAME out of `init/mcp.js`. The name is a wire identity — it is what this server calls itself in its `serverInfo` handshake — and living behind the installer meant the package's root entry point transitively reached the whole install-time subtree for one string. Moved to `@reticlehq/core` beside the other MCP wire constants; `init/` re-exports it, so every registration writer is untouched. A guard now walks the real import graph from the entry point and fails on any new edge into `init/`, with a counterpart walking from the CLI that requires the installer to still be reachable there — so the boundary can never be satisfied by deleting the thing it protects.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a lapsed customer could not be identified, which is most of what a lapse signal is for.** Found by running six dummy customers through a packaged release rather than the source tree: the expired one arrived UNATTRIBUTABLE. `licenseId` was reported only while a key verified AND was in date, so telemetry could say that somebody's licence had run out and never whose, and the renewal conversation still had to start with the customer noticing. An expired key is still SIGNED, so its claims are exactly as trustworthy as they were the day before and only the clock has moved; the id now rides it. The line that matters is the SIGNATURE, not the expiry, so a malformed or wrongly-signed key stays anonymous exactly as before, and that is pinned by its own test.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a paying customer could read `active` while every gated feature refused them.** From the same rehearsal: a key scoped to `sso` on a build that gates `audit-log` reported `active`, and then denied every gated call. Both halves were already on screen, `features` against `gated`, and nothing joined them, so the one word anybody actually reads said they were fine. `reticle license` now says outright when a valid key covers nothing the running build gates, and names both lists.

### Enterprise licensing

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a licence key could not have activated anything, because the issuer key never reached a release.** Activation verifies a signed key against an issuer public key compiled into the build, and the literal that holds it was empty with nothing anywhere stamping it. A published release therefore resolved no issuer key at all, which the gate correctly treats as a mis-built release and fails closed on: every enterprise feature would have denied in production with `enterprise-gate-unconfigured`, and every key we could have issued would have unlocked nothing. The stamp now runs inside `@reticlehq/server`'s `prepack`, and it has to: prepack opens with `rm -rf dist && tsc -b --force`, so a stamp applied anywhere earlier in a release job is deleted and rebuilt away with nothing having failed. That is not a theoretical ordering concern. The first version of this ran as a workflow step before `pnpm publish`, packed a tarball with an empty key, and reported success at every step; it was caught only by unpacking the artifact, so the ordering is now pinned by a test rather than a comment. The stamp refuses rather than no-ops on a malformed key, a private key pasted by mistake, or a missing literal, and re-imports the built module to confirm enforcement is live before a release continues.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — every key now carries a stable licence id, and the issuer records what it issued.** `org` is a display name typed at signing time, so two customers with the same name merged into one and a customer who renamed split into two. Keys carry `lid` instead, minted by the issuer and required rather than optional, because an optional identifier goes missing on exactly the keys somebody later needs to attribute; a key without one is re-issued rather than counted as nobody. `reticle license` reports it, so a customer can read their own id without being asked for anything. The issuer also appends every key it mints to a local ledger, which nothing did before: keys are stateless and the runtime never phones home, so a key handed over without its id written down was usage that could be neither attributed nor cleanly renewed. The ledger holds a fingerprint rather than the key, since a lost key is re-signed against the same id.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle license` was the one command that talks about licensing, and it was a dead end.** It told an interested reader to set an environment variable without saying what that would unlock or where to get a key, which is a dead end at exactly the moment somebody is asking. Every status now reports `gated` and `contact`, including the unlicensed branches, since the branch a prospective customer lands on is precisely the one that gets forgotten. `gated` is derived from the registry the gate itself reads rather than a second list somebody maintains, so it names what the running build will actually refuse instead of a roadmap: advertising features that are not there yet is how a buyer finds out on day two that the thing they paid for does not exist.

### Telemetry

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an event can say which licence it came from, and whether that licence still verifies.** A licensed deployment previously reported usage that was indistinguishable from every other install, so none of the questions a paying customer creates could be answered at all. `licenseId`, `licensePlan` and `licenseStatus` now ride the event envelope, resolved per event from the event's own clock rather than once at daemon start, because sessions here run for many hours and a key that expires mid-session must report `expired` from the event it expired on rather than the whole run inheriting what it had at boot. Status is deliberately separate from identity and keeps arriving through the failure states: `licenseId` exists only while a key verifies, so on identity alone a customer whose key lapsed and one who walked away are the same silence, and the renewal conversation starts after the customer notices instead of before. The organisation NAME never goes on the wire — it is free text somebody typed at signing time, and the contract is names, never values — so the id is opaque and resolves to a company only against the issuance ledger held locally, which means the analytics backend never holds a customer list. A build with no issuer key baked, which is every OSS install, sends none of the three, so an unlicensed payload is unchanged. `RETICLE_TELEMETRY=0` and `DO_NOT_TRACK` switch this off exactly as they switch off everything else: there is no exception for licensed installs, and adding one would put a hole in the kill switch that a security review is entitled to find. Because this changes what a licensed deployment sends, it is a term for the enterprise agreement rather than a quiet addition, and `docs/telemetry-contract.md` says so.

## [2.9.0] — 2026-08-20

### BREAKING — read before upgrading

- **The advertised tool surface is capped: 18 tools by default, 30 on `all`.** Editors budget MCP tools as a COUNT shared across every connected server — Cursor allows 40 in total — so advertising 48 from one server can push a user's other servers out or get this one dropped wholesale. Because the budget is a count and not a byte size, trimming parameter prose buys nothing back; only advertising fewer names does. `all` therefore stops meaning "every tool in the registry" and becomes the extended surface: the 18 default plus 12. Measured on the wire with a fresh daemon per reading, because the setting is read at daemon startup and a reused daemon reports the first surface it ever built — default 19 → 18 tools (21,307 → 20,783 B), `all` 48 → 30 tools (89,785 → 64,414 B). **Nothing became unreachable.** Everything omitted stays in the registry, stays catalogued by `reticle_tools` (verified: still 48 distinct names) and stays callable through `reticle_run { tool, args }` — checked by invoking four unadvertised tools and getting real payloads back, not by assuming it. `reticle_capabilities` leaves the default set: it was added as an explicit bet that orientation replaces exploratory snapshots, its own comment named the measurement that would settle that bet, and that measurement was never run — when the budget turns into a hard count, the unproven entry is the one that gives way. It is one `reticle_run` hop away and first in the extended set. What this costs, recorded rather than left to be discovered: `all` is the only surface carrying `outputSchema`, which is what makes the MCP layer validate tool OUTPUT, so the tools now outside `all` no longer get that validation from the wire.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an unscoped `reticle_state` returned the whole store tree, on every turn that followed.** A tool result is not paid once: it stays in the conversation and is re-sent on every later turn, so a reply costs its size multiplied by the turns after it. Measured on this repo's own fixture, one unscoped read returned 10,119 bytes — about 2,530 tokens, and roughly 34,000 across the rest of a 16-turn run for a single call. It was 81% of every tool payload measured; the next largest was 774 bytes. Bounded per store, the same read is 986 bytes — 90% smaller, with every store name, every top-level scalar and the component read exactly where they were. Only collections collapse, to markers that still say what is there (`deployments: "[Array(40)]"`, `filter: "{…2 keys}"`). Nothing became unreachable: an explicit `depth` is honoured at any value (9,891 bytes at depth 9) and `{ store, path }` reads a sub-tree at full fidelity for 314 bytes, so orient-then-drill costs ~300 tokens where the dump cost 2,530 and returns the value actually wanted rather than forty rows nobody read. The bounded reply also **says** it is bounded: an agent seeing `[Array(40)]` could otherwise take it for the store's contents and assert over a summary it never asked for — a green reached from evidence that was quietly withheld, which is the exact failure this product exists to prevent. `truncation` names the bound and the way past it, and appears only when something was actually trimmed, because a note on every reply is noise and noise is how a real disclosure stops being read.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a verification that names its own target is one call.** Measured on the wire, a targeted verification cost 11,235 tokens: 10,756 of them the advertised surface, 479 the actual answers. The bill was the menu, not the work. `reticle_act_and_wait` took only a `ref`, so every verification paid a `reticle_query` turn first purely to learn one string — and the surface is re-sent every turn, so that round trip cost half the run. It now accepts `target` (`{ testid }` | `{ text }` | `{ role, name }` | `{ label }`) and resolves it server-side; `reticle_act` takes the same argument. **Ambiguity refuses rather than picks:** choosing the first match would act on an element the caller never named and still report success, and the verdict would read correctly because it describes what it acted on — the exact false green this product exists to prevent. The refusal names the candidates so the caller can narrow, and hidden duplicates are filtered first, because a hidden twin is a common way for an unambiguous query to look ambiguous. End to end against the same fixture, verdict `yes / proved` throughout: 11,235 tokens over two turns → 5,909 in one call on the full surface.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a `verify` surface exists, stays off by default, and the measurement says why.** `verify` is one acting tool plus the two meta-tools that reach everything: 21,918 → 4,154 B of surface, and 1,469 tokens per verification. It is admitted on the same terms as `all` — named for what it does, never offered as a choice, off by default — and the reason it stays off is now measured rather than inherited. Run over the same 30 bugs the default surface scores 23/27 detection and 2/29 false alarms on: detection held (24/28) and tokens fell 37%, but **false alarms tripled, to 7/30**. Matched per bug, every one of the five new ones is a class whose evidence lives in `reticle_state`, `reticle_network` or `reticle_observe` — the tools this surface does not advertise. Strip the observation tools and the model stops observing: it reaches for a verdict without the evidence and calls a healthy build broken. 37% of the tokens is not worth the one metric this product measurably wins on — on that surface we would sit exactly level with the tool we beat. What survives unconditionally is `act_and_wait { target }`, which is one call instead of two on ANY surface and loses no evidence.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — shared arguments are documented once instead of on sixteen tools every turn.** The advertised surface is re-sent on every turn; the initialize `instructions` block is sent once — 621 tokens there against 5,480 per turn here, so a byte of prose moved between them is roughly 16x cheaper and the model still reads it. `sessionId` was the clearest case: byte-identical boilerplate on sixteen tools, re-explaining on every turn what it explained on the last one. With `since` and `limit` alongside it, the surface goes 21,918 → 20,551 bytes for +368 in instructions — a 6% saving on a run of any length. This removes REPETITION rather than evidence: every tool stays advertised, every observation stays one call away, and the short forms left behind still name each argument's purpose so a client that drops `instructions` is not left with a bare pointer.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the tool that reveals the other 30 tools described itself in five words.** The default surface trims every tool description to its first sentence, which is the right trade for a real tool and exactly the wrong one for `reticle_tools`: trimmed, it advertised as "Discover Reticle tools on demand." — naming no way to list anything, no parameter, and no hint that the visible 18 are 18 of 48. An agent had no reason to call it, so capping the advertised surface had quietly made the rest of the registry invisible rather than one hop away. Both meta-tools are now exempt from trimming on every surface, because their descriptions are not a description OF a capability, they ARE it. The catalog states its own size and that everything in it is callable whether advertised or not — an agent that can see 18 tools has no way to infer whether that is all of them, and being told the count is what turns "these are the tools" into "these are the tools I was shown". Costs +745 B (~186 tokens) per turn on the default surface: the price of the tail being reachable in practice rather than in principle.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle open` used a different daemon than you asked for and never said so.** The port is resolved leniently on purpose: if nothing is on the port you named it finds a running daemon instead, so nobody has to hunt for the port. That default is right and has one sharp edge — after substituting, every sentence the command prints is about a DIFFERENT daemon than the caller named. Found standing up a demo of this repo's own fixture: `open --port 4470` found nothing there, silently used a daemon on 4400, and reported that the app "carries no Reticle SDK, or dials a port other than 4400". The app was wired and was dialling 4470, exactly as asked; Reticle held both numbers and printed neither, so the one actionable fact was the only thing missing and the advice offered instead was to re-run `init` on a correctly installed app. The substitution is now stated first, with both numbers, before any cause that assumes they match, and it says outright that nothing below applies if the app dials the port that was asked for. Both ways out are offered — move the daemon or move the app — because which one is right depends on something only the caller knows. `requestedPort` is logged alongside `port` whenever they differ, so this is visible to anything parsing the event and not only to a human reading prose.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a dev server that was still compiling was reported as absent.** Reported from the field on Nuxt 4: the dev server was serving 57KB of HTML on port 5000, the reporter proved it answered on `127.0.0.1`, `::1` and `localhost`, and every Reticle diagnostic said "nothing is listening on the ports Reticle scans" and told them to start it — where a second `nuxt dev` would have hit the dev lock. The probe had accepted the connection and then given up waiting for the document, and that timeout was recorded as an absence. The 400ms budget is right for Vite handing back a static `index.html` and wrong for any server-rendered framework compiling a route on the first request. Fixed with a third state rather than a longer timeout, because a longer timeout would re-open the false positive this check exists to close: macOS AirPlay Receiver holds port 5000 on every Mac and ANSWERS, promptly, with 403 — still rejected on content, not on speed. A port that accepts a connection and then says nothing is no longer filed under "nothing is there"; it is a listener the probe cannot classify, which is evidence FOR the app being up. The diagnosis names those ports and says to open rather than start them, and says it BEFORE the sentence about nothing listening, because the reader acts on the first claim in the paragraph.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an agent with no shell was left with no way out at all.** Every remedy the no-session diagnosis offered on its never-connected branches was a shell command. Reported from Windows, where a half-failed plugin install left the MCP server registered and all its tools advertised while no `reticle` binary existed on disk, so the agent read the remediation, could not run any of it, and had no path forward; reported twice more by agents whose daemon was pinned to a different repo and were told to restart it. `reticle_lease { action: "acquire", url }` is that alternative and has been all along — it opens a browser Reticle drives itself and hands back a `sessionId`, with no CLI, no human and no shell — and it was named in three branches and missing from the ones an agent that has NEVER connected actually lands on. Two of those branches withheld it deliberately and that reasoning was right: a lease opens a URL, so offering one while nothing is listening is offering to open nothing. The fix is the ORDER rather than the offer — the lease is now named as what to do with the app's real URL once you have it, never as something to try immediately — and the wired-elsewhere branch, the most frequently reported of the batch, gets the full offer.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — "Send check-in" was blocked as destructive, and the remediation named a CLI that is usually absent.** Two field reports, both about Reticle taxing an agent that was doing nothing wrong. `send` was in the destructive classifier to cover moving money and caught every ordinary send-a-message, send-an-invite and send-feedback button with it, costing a round-trip each on a routine pass — and repeated false blocks train an agent to pass `confirmDangerous` reflexively, which is the one outcome that makes the guard worthless. Money is still covered, through the thing being SENT rather than the act of sending, which also closes a hole found in the other direction while pinning this: `Confirm payment` was NOT blocked, because `\bpay\b` does not match "payment". Both directions are asserted, because the guard is deliberately asymmetric — a false block costs a round-trip, a missed block can charge somebody's card. Separately, the no-session diagnosis told a blocked agent to run `reticle open <url>`, but Reticle registers its MCP server as `npx @reticlehq/server mcp`, so the ordinary install puts nothing named `reticle` on PATH. Every runtime remediation now names `npx @reticlehq/server …`, which runs from a bare npm environment; init-time messages keep the short form deliberately, since there the reader is already running the CLI.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the daemon opened whatever scheme it was handed.** `POST /drive` accepted any non-empty string as a url and passed it to the browser: `file:///etc/hosts`, `data:`, `javascript:` all answered 200 and were opened in a browser the daemon owns. Found by stress-testing the HTTP surface. Now an allowlist of `http:` and `https:`, checked before the browser is asked for anything, refusing with the scheme it got — the caller is a CLI printing this to somebody who typed the url and needs to see which part was the problem. Worth being accurate about the severity rather than dressing it up: the route already requires a JSON content type, which forces a preflight no browser completes without our opt-in, so a web page cannot reach this, and the caller who can is a local process that already has broader access than this check removes. It is hygiene about what the daemon will open on request, not a security boundary.

- **`docs/benchmarks.md` — the 88-bug comparison was measured and never published.** The page led with "ten injected regressions" while the largest measurement in the repo compared 88 seeded bugs against a Playwright script and lived only in `bench/`. These are two different opponents answering two different questions, so the 88-bug result is added as its own section rather than replacing the ten: the ten-bug table is Playwright MCP and Chrome DevTools MCP, agent-driven, measuring token cost per look; the 88-bug table is a Playwright script with no model in the loop, measuring what each tool can structurally see. The honesty already in the scorecard carries over — the two uncaught bugs are the registry's false-positive traps, where 0 of 2 is the correct score and catching them would be worse, and the tie categories are named, because Reticle ties on everything observable from outside the app and wins only the inside-the-app classes.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a page that no longer exists could contradict a verdict about the page that does.** Reported from the field with unusually complete evidence: an agent reloaded the app, restarted its API server, then asserted four strings that were genuinely rendered. Every clause passed, and the verdict came back `verified: "no"`, contradicted, blaming hundreds of failed requests — all of them 500s, none of them live. The reporter proved that three ways: their own server log recorded no server errors since the restart, one failing URL named a database row that does not exist, and the failures predated both a hard reload and the restart. They then had to reconstruct the real answer from the raw pass flag and their own logs, which is the point at which a verdict has stopped being one. The cause was the window rather than the rules: an assertion defaults to judging from the last thing the agent did, and only acting moved that mark — navigating, by URL or by reload, did not. Since the event history is durable and outlives the document, an assert after a reload judged the whole session, and failures belonging to the destroyed page were still in it. A navigation now moves that mark on both paths, recorded before the page goes away — fixed at the window, because every contradiction rule reads it, so the alternative was leaving the next rule to rediscover the same thing. Stated precisely, because the difference matters: the unbounded window is real and is closed, but the reported symptom could not be reproduced on this build — driven end to end, a reload already leaves the window empty, so the report's own path needs something this reproduction did not capture.
