The change is therefore a narrowing of what a verdict may draw on, and not a demonstrated cure for that report.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — the same text, written two legal ways, did not match itself.** Unicode lets `café` be one code point or two — `é`, or `e` followed by a combining acute. They render identically, they are canonically equivalent, and JavaScript string comparison says they are different, so a query typed one way against a page holding the other found nothing. That is the worst direction for this product to be wrong in: the agent asserts text that is visibly on the screen, is told the element is not there, and reports a working app as broken, while the person reading the report is looking straight at the text. It cannot happen in English, which is why it survived — every fixture in this project is English. It is ordinary in French, Vietnamese and Korean, and the decomposed form is what macOS filesystems, several input methods and some databases hand back. Both sides are now normalised for every query over user-visible text — text, label, placeholder and alt — composed with the existing whitespace handling rather than replacing it. This can only ever add matches: normalisation is idempotent and preserves canonical equivalence, so anything that matched before still matches. Deliberately stopping at a canonical fold and not a compatibility one: a fullwidth `１` still does not match an ASCII `1`, because that is different text rather than a different form, and matching it would trade a false red for a false green.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a timed-out tool call was retried as though the page had been replaced, and one oversized line froze the link.** Two separate ways the agent's connection stopped answering, neither of which killed the daemon, which from the agent's side is the same thing. The rebinding added for a page that reconnects under the same id fired on ANY failure, so a command the page had simply not answered in time was read as "you were replaced, ask again" — against a page reloading in a loop that turned one read into hundreds of commands on the wire and an error still quoting a budget it had exceeded many times over. A timeout and a lost transport mean opposite things: one says the page was asked and did not answer, the other says the question never arrived. Separately, the reader that assembles messages from the input stream re-scanned everything it held on every chunk, so a single very large line cost time proportional to the square of its size and pinned the process for tens of seconds — every other tool call on that link hanging with no response and no error. There is now a ceiling on a single line, and a line past it is discarded rather than accumulated; the rest of that discarded line is swallowed too, because without that its tail arrives looking like a complete message and is forwarded as a fragment nobody sent.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a consequence that could be answered from the past could no longer pass on its own.** The `route` predicate gained a fallback to where the app currently is, so that "did the session survive a reload?" is answerable at all. That made a route consequence satisfiable by a state that predated the action: an app already on `/login`, a click whose handler is broken, nothing navigating, and an assertion that the action reached `/login` passing on the strength of it having been there all along. `route` is now treated as reading live state, so the same before-check every other state predicate gets applies to it, and a consequence that already held is reported as `already_true` — an `unknown` with the reason — rather than a pass.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a page that reconnected twice left the caller talking to a session that was already gone.** When a page re-dials under the same id, the displaced session is handed its replacement so a call already in flight can finish against the live connection. Two reloads in quick succession make a chain, and the handle the caller holds then points at a session that is itself displaced, with the open page one hop further on — so the rebinding stopped at the first closed socket and handed back exactly the error it exists to remove. It now walks to the live session, bounded, so a page re-dialling in a loop cannot turn the walk into a hang.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — two refusals that left no trace anywhere.** A browser turned away because too many connections were mid-handshake, and a session whose id cannot be used as a directory name and is therefore never journalled. In both cases the app was running, instrumented and actively trying, and every surface a reader can check said the same thing an app nobody started would say — so the diagnosis went looking for a stopped dev server. Both now record what happened where the no-session diagnosis already reads, with wording that says the app is not the problem. The journal case is the quieter of the two: the session connects and drives normally, and the only difference is that its durable record does not exist, so every query that reads back through it returns nothing.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `doctor` could not say the app was never instrumented.** It already read `.reticle.json` for the port, so it knew whether the file was there, and never mentioned it. In a project that has not been through `init` the checklist came out clean — node fine, browser fine, a daemon that starts on demand, a bridge port, no port mismatch — with the one thing actually wrong going unremarked. That is the state `doctor` gets run to explain. It now carries a line about the project, and it tells a corrupt config from a missing one, because saying a file is absent while it sits in front of the reader sends them hunting the wrong thing. A project that has connected here before is reported as wired whatever the file says, since a build plugin can instrument an app without writing one.

- **Reticle no longer leaves session journals in your `git status`.** The workspace directory is created by the daemon rather than by you, so it arrives untracked and unexplained — and it deliberately mixes two kinds of file: the contract, saved flows and baselines are meant to be committed, while session journals and learned state are per-machine churn. Nothing separated them, so a single `git add -A` could put journals into a shared repository, and those carry URLs, request and response bodies and page text from the app under test. Reticle now writes an ignore for the half it owns, inside the directory it created. It never touches your own `.gitignore`, and it writes this one once, so an edited copy is never silently reverted.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` could report success over an app that can never connect, four different ways.** The confirmation that joins "init finished" to "an app connected" is skipped when nobody is at the terminal, which is right — blocking a script for twelve seconds is not — but it also said nothing at all, and an agent running `init` through a shell is never a terminal. That is the path every skill and every README block prescribes, so most people who ran `init` were left with files on disk, an exit code of 0, and no indication that the page half had not happened. It still does not wait; it now says so and names the command that proves it, and it claims nothing it did not check — it did not look for a session, so it does not say whether one exists. Separately, two steps that decide whether the app can dial the daemon were not counted towards the exit code: mounting the dev component into a root layout `init` cannot patch (the component is written, never rendered, and nothing imports it), and the CRA pairing token, without which the bridge refuses every connection so the app boots, looks correct, and never pairs. And `reticle status` — the command `init` tells you to run next — hardcoded "this project is initialized", so the one branch that can say "run `init` here" was unreachable from it however unwired the project was.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the one thing Reticle says unprompted was withheld from the people it was written for, and on the commonest path was never sent at all.** The MCP handshake carries a block that opens "no app has ever connected to Reticle in this project — having these tools is not the same as being set up". That is a claim about a project, and it was decided with a question about a port: a project with no config has no project id, which fell through to "has any app connected on this port", true on any machine where that port has ever served anything. So the guidance was suppressed for exactly the installs it addresses. Worse, when no daemon is up yet the proxy answers the handshake itself, and that response carried no instructions field whatsoever — and a client reads that field once, at connect. Since answering locally is what happens on a first run, the block was not merely suppressed there but absent, and the daemon's own correct instructions, sent whenever it later arrived, reached a client that would never read the field again.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a page that reconnected under the same id turned the next tool call into an error.** Reported repeatedly, across clients: a navigation succeeds, and the snapshot immediately after it fails with "session replaced by a newer connection claiming the same id". The bridge is right — the page really did re-dial, and the newcomer should win — but every call already on the wire was rejected, and the id the caller should use next was in the daemon's hand and never returned. Each of those reports ends the same way: an extra listing call and a retry, to rediscover an id that had not changed. A displaced session is now handed its replacement before it is closed. A call that had not yet been sent goes to the live connection, because nothing was on the wire and nothing can run twice; a read that was already in flight is re-issued against it. An action that was already in flight still errors, deliberately — it may have been dispatched in the page that went away, and repeating it behind the caller's back is a double submit nobody asked for.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a lookup was graded as a write whose fields had been ignored.** The echo check gated on the response being a success and never on the request being a write, so a read that sends its key in the body and gets a record back — a POST that fetches — was reported as a half-applied save, on the strength of a key name that legitimately means different things on the two sides of a request and its response. Nothing had been written. It now considers only requests the product already treats as mutating, sharing the single definition of that rather than adding a second, and findings are attributed against the action being verified so one about a request that had already settled says so instead of reading as a verdict on the change you just made. The narrower case of a read-shaped POST is unchanged and deliberately so: by method alone it cannot be told from a write, and the rule that would silence it would also silence a genuinely dropped field, which is a missed bug in somebody's app.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — three ways a correct app was told it was broken.** An unknown key inside an element query was stripped rather than refused, so a query naming a selector Reticle does not accept became an empty query, and the verdict read "no element matched {}" about an element that was on the page — an assertion of absence, produced from a typo, which an agent then acts on by "fixing" code that was already right. The query is now strict, as the predicate around it already was, and names the key. A `route` predicate could only ever be satisfied by a navigation inside the window, so "did the session survive a reload" — which by definition produces no route change — and "we landed on /login" after a completed navigate were both guaranteed failures, contradicted by Reticle's own status read one call later; it now falls back to where the app actually is, and says which of the two decided it, because a redirect and a page that was already there are different claims. And a network count failure printed its filter with the body clause removed while still applying it, so a response whose body did not match was reported as a request that never fired — the first user of body assertions was sent hunting a wiring bug that did not exist.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — `upload` invented a file and reported success, and the destructive-action guard read the whole form.** Every field of the uploaded file was defaulted, so a call whose arguments used a key `upload` does not read built a small placeholder text file, uploaded that, and returned ok. The server answered 200, the interface refreshed, and every signal available to the agent said the document had been processed. It now refuses when nothing it reads was supplied, and when a key was supplied that it would silently drop — the second is the more dangerous of the two, because the right filename arriving attached to invented bytes is harder to notice than no file at all. Separately, the check that blocks a potentially destructive action classified the entire enclosing form's text, so any form containing per-row "Remove" buttons made every control inside it — including its own submit — read as destructive, intermittently, depending on whether those rows had rendered. The cost is not the blocked click: the reported workaround was to pass the override on every call, which removes the guard entirely.

- **Telemetry: the event that reports an install which never got an app could not see the case it exists for.** It was only reported from the periodic flush, which refuses until a generous wait has passed — but a daemon with no agent attached shuts itself down well before that, so it was gone before it was allowed to speak. Only a daemon with an agent attached lived long enough, which meant the "an agent was attached" field was true on essentially every record and the outcome the event was built to describe — installed it, walked away, never wired the app — could not appear at all. It is now also reported when the daemon exits, where the wait no longer applies: a daemon that is ending and never saw an app has stalled, and that is a fact rather than an inference. The duration is still carried, so a short run can be excluded on the data instead of being invisible.

- **`@reticlehq/vite-plugin`, `@reticlehq/server` — Reticle's own journal was reloading the page that was writing it.** Reported from the field with the causal chain established rather than guessed. The daemon keeps its session journal inside the project, and rewrites one file in it continuously while a session is live. That directory sits in the tree the dev server watches and is not excluded by default, so every journal write arrived as a project file changing and the dev server answered the only way it can — a full page reload. Which closes a loop with no exit: the page loads, the SDK connects and streams events, the daemon journals them, the page reloads, the SDK reconnects. Several times a second, for as long as the dev server is up, and worse the more Reticle is used. None of the symptoms pointed here: element references went stale between the call that minted one and the call that used it, actions died mid-flight as the document was torn down under them, and the log filled with connect and disconnect pairs that read as an SDK unable to hold a socket rather than a watcher chasing its own tail. The plugin now excludes that directory from the watcher, appending to whatever the app already excludes. Astro is patched the same way, because it is Vite-based but renders its own HTML and never loads the plugin; Nuxt, which owns its own Vite instance and has no config for the plugin to reach, gets it as a numbered step in the recipe `init` prints. It is a regular expression rather than a glob, and that is the difference between the fix working and not: the glob form is what Vite documents and uses for its own defaults, and it matches nothing on the file watcher recent Vite versions ship — accepted in silence, visible in the resolved configuration, and changing no behaviour at all.

- **`SKILL.md` and the published skills now lead with the calls that answer a question in one hop.** The skill ended setup by telling the agent to snapshot, act, assert and read state — a call per interaction. Every call is a full model turn, and in a client that asks the user to approve each one it is also a click, so a flow driven that way is how somebody gives up before they ever see a verdict. It now batches the setup into one call, produces the verdict with one more, and ends by saving the flow, so the next check of the same journey is a single call with no model in the loop. It also names the two tools that answer the commonest questions outright — "did my edit break anything" and "does this known journey still work" — which are not on the advertised tool list and are therefore invisible to an agent that is not told they exist. What `unknown` means when nothing has been recorded yet is now stated where it will be read, because a first-time user pointed at that answer reads "nothing was checked" as a failure.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — "the port is open" was still being used to mean "a daemon is there" on the path agents take.** The distinction exists precisely because a boolean answer lies, and every human-facing command had been converted to ask both questions — is the port open, and does it answer as a daemon. The agent path had not, so a port held by something that was not Reticle was reported as a healthy daemon, and the resulting failure surfaced as a disconnection with no cause. This is the shared root of a family of reports about drops "for no reason".

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a verdict said nothing changed when nothing had been watching.** One action is meant to show its whole effect — network, console, storage, state — without a follow-up call. All four were carried, but an unwatched channel could not be told from a quiet one: an app with no subscribable store returned an empty set of state differences, which reads as "state did not change" and means "nothing was observing state". A confident wrong answer, in the one place this product sells certainty.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a failing assertion no longer spends a budget that cannot change the answer.** Passing verdicts return quickly; failing ones sit for the whole timeout the caller granted, which is where slow verdicts come from. Most of that is inherent and stays — proving something did not happen requires waiting, and ending early would manufacture false negatives, which is the one trade this codebase does not make for speed. The exception is an exact count, which only ever accumulates: once more matches have arrived than were asked for, nothing can bring the number back down, so the answer is final and the wait ends there. Narrow, and it is the double-submit case, which is the highest-value finding and was the slowest to report.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — `check` and `uncheck` lost their attribution, their cancellation and their radio invariant.** Mostly the work of **@snowyukitty** in #383, whose review of this path found three things the original fix missed. Those actions activate through a real click, so they need everything a click needs — the point geometry, the off-viewport scroll, the occlusion test and the component and source attribution — and they were not being treated as click-like, so a verdict about one came back without the file and line that makes a finding actionable. A cancelled event no longer reports that the state changed, and selecting a radio again reflects that its siblings are now deselected.

- **`SKILL.md` — a variant for agents with nobody to ask.** The install skill hands control back twice: restart your client so it picks up the MCP server, and open the app in a browser. Both are correct when a person is sitting there, and both are dead ends in an autonomous agent — the restart ends the context holding the instructions, and there is nobody to open a tab. An agent that follows them stops with the tools connected and nothing verified, which is the shape of most abandoned installs.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` registers Antigravity, and registers OpenCode where OpenCode actually reads.** `init` did not know Antigravity at all; users hand-wired a registration, got that far, and then never ran `init`, never instrumented an app, and never called a tool — nothing in that state tells anyone there is a second half to the install. OpenCode was listed as project-scoped, and registration was gated on the marker file already existing, so a project that had never written one was never wired: the client connects and then nothing happens, which from outside is indistinguishable from a client that fails to drive.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the install no longer opens with somebody else's security warnings.** The dependency install is a child process whose output lands above ours, so a run could open with a funding notice and a wall of vulnerability advice about the user's own existing dependency tree — which two dev-only packages neither caused nor can fix — before a single line of Reticle output, at exactly the moment somebody is deciding whether to trust it.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `status` answers the question `init` sends you there to ask.** `init` closes by telling you to run it, saying it "confirms the app connected, or says exactly why it has not". It printed a couple of fields and no reason. It is the last checkpoint in the setup path, so somebody who arrives and learns nothing has nowhere left to go.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — assert on what the server answered, not merely that it answered.** Reported by an agent driving a real payments interface: a refund posted an amount, the server read the number in a different unit and answered 200 having refunded a hundredth of it, and the page rendered the amount the user had typed rather than the amount that came back. Every channel that could be asserted was green — the request fired, exactly once, status 200, console clean, page settled — so the verdict was a pass over a refund that was wrong by two orders of magnitude. A status code describes the transport and not the result. Response bodies are now recorded by default on a fresh install, because a write that answers 2xx with a body nobody captured can only grade as unknown, which made the bug class this product exists to catch unreachable on a default install; and an assertion can name what the response must contain, so the check is against the server's answer rather than against what the interface chose to display.

- **`@reticlehq/vite-plugin` — an app without `"type": "module"` could not start at all.** Vite bundles the config as CommonJS and requires it, and this package shipped only an ESM entry, so the import `init` writes could not be loaded, the config failed, and the dev server refused to start. The app served nothing until the user deleted our line — and from where they were sitting, Reticle had broken their project. Every Vite surface we own sets that field, and the install gate scaffolds with a tool that emits it, so this needed an app older than anything we generate and was structurally invisible to every check we had.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — three false greens: an empty gate, an unreachable flag, and a check that dispatched nothing.** Coverage reported a pass at a hundred percent over nothing at all, and agents are told to run it to learn whether a change is covered — so one that saw green reported "the covered flows still pass", using our own output as the evidence. A fresh project, a broken install and a real passing suite all looked identical. An empty gate is now its own outcome and cannot be read as a pass.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a wired app was told it was not wired.** Several reports, several apps, one shape: the port was right every time, re-running `init` would have been the wrong action every time, and the hint led with a port mismatch anyway. Reporters spent tool calls disproving it; one recovered the real rule by reading our compiled output inside `node_modules`; several gave up and told their human Reticle was not set up, about a project that was fully set up. The daemon already held the evidence against its own hint — it said an app had connected here before, and then proposed a port mismatch seconds later — and the richest diagnosis it had was being discarded in exactly the case where it knew the most.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a consequence the caller declared, and that held, is no longer overruled by settlement.** Idle settlement was never "the page went quiet": it is whether the browser painted within a short window, so a hidden or backgrounded tab never satisfies it — and a backgrounded tab is the normal state for a page an agent drives while its human works elsewhere. A signal that is always false in the usual operating mode cannot be a precondition for a verdict, and it was vetoing proofs: the caller named the consequence before acting, the consequence held, the evidence was in the payload, and the answer came back `unknown`. Two further paths fed the same wrongness, including an app that polls, where a request is always outstanding and the "never settled" finding was therefore permanent.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the MCP handshake now tells an uninstrumented project to instrument, before teaching it the tool grammar.** That string is the only thing Reticle says unprompted, and it is the one channel that reaches an install which arrived through a plugin listing or a pasted configuration block, with no skill file and no action from the user. It opened on tool grammar — the right thing to say to an agent that has an app to point the tools at, and the wrong thing to say to one that does not.

- **`SKILL.md` — the paste-URL route could not survive its own restart.** The skill told the agent to resume at the step it was on after restarting the client. On that route the instruction cannot be obeyed: the restart ends the context holding the file, and the agent that comes back has never read it, sees a configuration file and working tools, and reasonably concludes the job is done — with nothing instrumented and nothing verified.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — one malformed request line could kill the daemon, and the outage metric could not say so.** `GET //` was enough. The request target was parsed at the top of the request listener, outside any try/catch, and Node's HTTP parser accepts targets the URL parser rejects; a synchronous throw there is an uncaught exception, which the daemon answers by exiting. The parse also sat before the authorization gate it fed, so nothing had to be trusted to send it. Because it is a process death rather than a dropped connection, it explains a detail in the field reports that nobody would invent: an unrelated session, on another app and another port, dies at the same instant. The socket is destroyed without a clean close, so the client sees an aborted stream; the next call wakes a fresh daemon with a new process id; and that daemon has never connected anything, so it reports that it has never seen a session moments after its predecessor served several. Two further paths that could exit the process were closed with it. Separately, the metric that was supposed to measure connection loss could not describe one: most of what it counted was the daemon's own scheduled idle exit — the product working as designed, recorded as a fault — and its stage field was emitted from a point where it could only ever hold one value, so the event had a single degree of freedom. The daemon now announces its retirement before closing, so a planned shutdown is classified as one, and a recovery reports the real number of attempts.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — a redirect is not a failed request, and both transports now agree.** The `fetch` path reported the browser's own success flag straight through, which is true only for 200–299, so every 3xx arrived stamped as a failure — while the `XHR` path one file over computed the same field itself and called the identical status a success. Downstream that field is authoritative, so the two did not merely disagree; the wrong one won. A POST-redirect-GET login, which is the first flow anyone verifies in an app with authentication, produced a contradiction citing its own success path, and the agent's only recourse was to read the statuses back by hand and overrule the verdict in prose to its human. A verdict that gets manually overruled is not deciding anything.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — on Tauri, Reticle reported its own screenshots as writes your app had made, three times over.** A Tauri app has no preload stage, so a screenshot reaches the shell the only way it can: an `invoke`. And an `invoke` is not a special channel — it is a real `fetch` to Tauri's `ipc://` protocol, travelling straight through the patch the network observer had just installed. So the observer recorded itself: every capture appeared in `reticle_network` as an `ipc://` call the app never made, and because a desktop IPC call counts as a mutating method, three captures taken inside one action window came back as `duplicate-request` — "one user action was performed, the same write fired 3 times" — against an app whose only fault was being looked at. Accusing correct code of a double submit is the most damaging direction of error a verification tool has, and `duplicate-request` is the finding we report most, so this was worth more than the number of captures involved. Reticle's own desktop traffic is now skipped before it is observed at all, neither pending nor completed, which also stops a screenshot from counting as a request in flight while a settle wait is running. Electron never had the problem, because its preload has always taken its captures through the unpatched channel for exactly this reason, and the bridge's own socket has always been skipped on the same principle; this was the third instrument that was measuring itself, and the only one nobody had noticed. The app's own commands are observed exactly as before — one record each, verdict and all — and a genuine double submit is still two records and still reported.

- **Telemetry: a new event reports an install that never got an app.** Reticle already reported the moment an app first connects to a daemon; it had nothing to say about the far more common case where one never does, so that outcome existed in the data only as an absence, which cannot be told apart from a process that died before it could report. `instrumentation_stalled` fires at most once per daemon run, only after a generous wait, and never once an app has connected, so a slow start that eventually works reports nothing at all. It carries the same three facts as its success counterpart and no more: whether `init` had run here, whether an agent was attached, and how long the wait was. No path, no URL, no project detail, and deliberately no diagnosis, because working out WHY nothing connected means probing your machine, and probing to enrich a metric is collecting for the metric's sake. It respects `RETICLE_TELEMETRY=0` and `DO_NOT_TRACK` like everything else.

- **The agent now starts the dev server when nothing is serving the app.** Every document that instructs an agent used to say the opposite — `SKILL.md` put it as "Reticle never starts the dev server for you, that's your job" — and agents obeyed it exactly: they read the rule, declined to start anything, and ended the turn with nothing verified. An instruction that reliably produces no verification is a defect in the instruction. The agent, not the daemon: a build process spawned by a long-lived background daemon is invisible to whoever's machine it runs on, was never consented to, and orphans when the daemon exits, whereas an agent already runs install and build commands in the repo under a host that asks the human first. So the rule is now: read the project's own dev script from `package.json`, run it in the background, say in one line that it is running and how to stop it. Five guards ship with it wherever it appears — never start a second one, never guess the command, never kill anything, always background it and say so, and the permission prompt stays the agent host's. The corollary is unchanged and now stated next to it: when a dev server IS running and nothing connects, the cause is the SDK not loading in the page, and telling the user to start what they already started ends the conversation with nothing fixed. Updated in the skill, the rule `init` writes into `CLAUDE.md` / `AGENTS.md` / the Cursor rule, the `/reticle` command, the no-session recovery hint, and the docs.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — "no browser session connected" was a paragraph, and the agent needed a command.** The daemon has been able to tell the cases apart for a while — nothing running, something running that was never wired, a wired app with no page open, a tab that went away — and it said so in careful prose that an agent then had to translate into an action it could actually take. `reticle_sessions` now returns that same answer a second way, as a structured `next_action` carrying the literal command and the port. The command is read from the project's own `package.json` scripts and its lockfile, so it is `pnpm run dev` in a pnpm repo and `npm run start` in a repo whose only script is `start`; when the project declares no dev script at all, it says so and returns no command rather than handing back a guess, because a command that does not exist sends the agent chasing an error in its own repo. The four cases get four different actions, and none of them is a start-the-dev-server command while a dev server is already listening.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the app was already running and Reticle reported absence anyway.** The commonest near-miss in the whole funnel: a dev server is up, nothing has opened a page, and the one thing standing between the agent and a verdict is a browser nobody opened. When the port scan finds exactly one listening dev server and the project is wired, the daemon now opens that URL itself, in a browser it already owns — the same one `reticle_lease` uses, inside the daemon, so it competes with nothing for a port. It is deliberately automatic in that case and only that case: a wired project means a page will connect, and one listening server means there is no question which. Several listeners, an unwired project, or none at all still return the diagnosis, because there Reticle would be guessing and a guess that opens a browser is worse than a sentence. It is attempted once per port for the life of a daemon and never retried, and if it fails the message says so and names the reason instead of reporting plain absence.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the escape hatch Reticle recommends could not run while Reticle was running.** `reticle drive <url>` is what the session recommendation points at when a tab is hidden or throttled and cannot be scripted — and it bound the bridge port itself, which the daemon already holds the moment any agent connects. So the one command offered as a way out died with a raw `EADDRINUSE` in exactly the state that makes it necessary, and the documented workaround made it worse: stopping the daemon frees the port, then the MCP proxy respawns one into the gap before `drive` can bind, so the retry loses a race the user has no way to win. Reported twice from the field, a week apart; one of the two gave up and drove a browser over raw CDP by hand instead. Driving is now a request to the daemon that is already there rather than a second process competing with it: it hands back a fresh isolated, never-throttled context — the same one an agent gets from a lease — and the session lives in that daemon, so the tools the agent already has open can address it by id. Nothing is stopped, nothing is bound, and there is no race left to lose. A port held by something that is not a Reticle daemon is still refused, now by naming the holder and the way out; `EADDRINUSE` is no longer a thing this command can show anybody.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an error path could never be verified, because declaring the failure was read as a contradiction.** Verifying error handling means declaring a failing request in the oracle — `{ net, POST, /api/login, status: 500 }` — plus the right message on screen and the wrong one absent. Every clause passed, with full evidence, on every branch of a login error path, and every run still came back `verified: "no"`, `contradicted`, because "channels disagree about this action (ui-advanced-request-failed)". That rule cannot tell an app that swallowed an error from an app that rendered one: both are a moved DOM beside a failed request. It recovered the difference from the app's own state, which an app that renders its error straight into the DOM never touches. The caller had already said what was expected, before acting, in the predicate — and nothing downstream read it. It does now, so a declared failure is an expectation rather than a disagreement. The result is that error handling — empty states, offline banners, 4xx/5xx messaging, lockouts, rate limits — is verifiable for the first time, which matters because it is the code most worth verifying and it was the least able to reach a green. Deliberately the narrowest fix that works: it silences only the rule that cannot see the difference. An app that fires a success signal over the declared failure still contradicts, and one that blames the user for a 5xx is still reported as misattributing it.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a verdict about your app was decided by your dev toolchain's own network traffic.** Next's dev overlay resolves a source map with a `POST` the moment the app logs one React warning, and that request answers 200 with a JSON body nobody captures. The rule that says "a write returned 2xx and its body went unread, so the one channel that could disagree was never opened" read that as the app's own write — so on a Next dev app, one stray React warning could make every subsequent verdict `unknown`, on traffic the app did not make. The contradiction engine has excluded toolchain traffic since the same overlay was turning correct navigations red; this check and the `202 Accepted` check were reading the unsplit window. Both now apply the same exclusion. The caveat also names the write that caused it — method and URL — instead of describing a shape, because a caveat you cannot locate is one you learn to skip.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an `unsettled` verdict named a clause the evidence beside it disproved.** A navigation whose destination heading was found, with its source file and line, came back `unknown` because "this window closed before the app finished (route-rendered-nothing)" — a blank-destination finding sitting directly beside proof that the destination rendered. That finding is inferred from the absence of DOM events, and positive evidence outranks the absence it is inferred from, so a declared on-screen consequence that held now settles the question. A route change nobody asserted content for is still reported exactly as before, since nothing then witnesses the destination. The second half of the same report: when a verdict is downgraded to `unsettled` with nothing left in flight, it used to blame unnamed churn — "a poll, a timer, an animation" — which is unactionable on an app whose actual problem is one endpoint being retried against a dead backend, since every attempt completes and nothing is ever outstanding. It now names the endpoints that fired repeatedly, so "my assertion was wrong" can be told apart from "this app never quiets".

- **`@reticlehq/vite-plugin` — the whole page reloaded about once a second, forever, until the plugin was removed.** Reported on a Vite + React app on a non-default bridge port: after wiring, every route reloaded roughly once a second and never finished settling, so the app showed blank. The plugin serves its injected connect module fresh so that a daemon started _after_ the dev server still gets a credentialled connect — but it did that by dropping the module from Vite's graph on **every** request for it, including the request each reload makes. A module force-invalidated on every load is re-resolved from scratch on every load, which is the shape a reload loop needs to sustain itself: reload, request, invalidate, re-resolve, reload. It is now dropped only when the module's source would actually differ, which is the same rule stated honestly — the late daemon's token appearing _is_ a difference, so nothing that worked stops working, and once the port and token settle the module goes inert. If some input never settles, the plugin now says so by name once instead of leaving a silently reloading page as the only symptom.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an install can finish all-green and leave the agent talking to another project's daemon.** When `init` wires an app in a subdirectory, `.reticle.json` went there and only there. Nothing in the app reads that file: the CLI and `reticle mcp` read it from their OWN working directory, which is the repo root the agent runs from. So the root had no config, `reticle mcp` fell back to the default bridge port, and on a machine with more than one instrumented repo — the normal case — that port belongs to somebody else's daemon, which would have answered `reticle_sessions` with a different app's tabs while the wired app sat unseen. The same config is now written where the agent stands as well as in the app, byte-identical so the two can never disagree about the port or the project identity, and it is a named step in the plan rather than something the reader has to infer.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` reported an MCP registration as already wired when it pointed at the wrong package.** A registration naming `@reticlehq/core` — the wire contract, which has no MCP server in it — is not a registration; the client shows the server errored with zero tools, and every `reticle_*` tool is unavailable until somebody edits that config by hand. Idempotency only asked whether an entry existed, so `init` printed `·` and moved on, on every re-run. An entry naming any Reticle package is now ours to repair, in every client config `init` writes and not only the one it was reported on. An entry that names no Reticle package at all — somebody pointing `reticle` at their own local build — is still a deliberate choice and is still left exactly as it is.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the daemon said a project had never been through `init` while its config sat right there.** Whether a project is wired was decided once, when the daemon booted — and the ordinary first-install order is the other way round: something starts a daemon, then `init` writes the config, then the app is wired. The answer was then permanently stale, so `reticle status` sent people to re-run an install that was already done, when the real cause was a dev server older than the plugin. It is now read when the question is asked, which is what the port half of the same message already did, and the reader lands on the diagnosis that names restarting the dev server.

- **`@reticlehq/core` — importing the browser SDK from it blanked the app with a `SyntaxError`.** `core` is the wire contract and has no DOM in it, but it is the package whose name reads like the thing you install, so `import { install, reticle } from '@reticlehq/core'` keeps getting written. The module has no such exports, so the page went blank with an error naming neither the mistake nor the fix. The package is not renamed; the names now exist and say where the browser API actually lives, which turns a blank page into a sentence.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the one call an agent is told it MUST make failed whenever no app was attached.** `reticle_session { action: "yield" }` is how an agent hands control back between turns, and the tool description calls it mandatory. It resolved a browser session in order to update the panel, so with nothing connected it threw instead — on much the most common state a daemon is in. The reported consequence is the bad one: agents stop calling it and write "Reticle had no session" into their prose, which leaves the panel showing an agent that walked away, and it made this the single largest source of refusals we see. Ending a turn is a statement about the agent, not about a tab. With nothing attached there is no panel to update, so yield and end now succeed and say that in a note, rather than failing a call whose whole job is to report that the agent has stopped. Naming a session that does not exist is still an error, because that is a mistake about which tab you meant.

- **`@reticlehq/test` — every TypeScript spec failed to compile on its first matcher.** The `t` handed to a spec body was typed as a context carrying only `invoke`, on the reasoning that the runner treats it as opaque and should not depend on the matchers. That is true of the runner and false of the person writing the spec: at runtime `t` has always been the full context, so a spec written exactly as documented ran correctly and would not type-check, reporting that `expectText` does not exist. Nothing caught it because this package's own spec file is `.mjs`, which type-checks none of it, and no runtime test can see a problem that only exists in the types. The public type now describes what a spec actually receives. The runner still never builds a context, so its decoupling is unchanged; it no longer describes that context to callers as less than it is.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — standing one directory away from the config looked exactly like never having installed.** Every reader of `.reticle.json` joined the name onto its own working directory and stopped there, so a process that started anywhere but the exact folder holding the file got no port, no project identity and no journal setting, and the diagnostics that follow from that told the user to run an install they had already run. It is the same missing step behind several different-looking reports: an app wired in `frontend/` while the agent runs from the repo root, a package deep inside a monorepo, a git worktree checked out beside its main repo. The nearest config at or above the working directory is now the one that answers, so the closest project still wins and a bounded walk keeps a process in an unrelated folder from quietly adopting a distant project's port. It does not make a worktree share the daemon bound to its main checkout, which is a separate question about which app you meant.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a desktop app was told it had ignored a response it had just handled.** `response-ignored` waits for the app to react before it accuses, because the re-render lands a task or two after the write resolves. That grace asks "was this a successful mutating request?" — and it answered from its own copy of the method list, which had been written out by hand and was missing `IPC`. The accuser's copy was not. So on Tauri and Electron, where every write is an IPC call, the grace was never paid: the window closed in the gap between the response and the render, and a correct app was reported as having dropped its own write. Both now read the one list, which is the rule this codebase already has about restating a constant, arriving as a correctness bug rather than a style note.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a check that correctly found nothing looked like a check that had not run.** Asserting that a control is absent, scoped by role and name, returned a bare pass with no evidence of its own, so the payload fell back to listing everything matching the outer query. Three clauses asking about three different names then came back byte-identical, and identical answers to different questions is the signature of a dropped argument — so the honest verdict read as a broken one, and the agent that hit it stopped believing the result and re-read the evidence array by hand. A passing negation now carries what it looked for and that it was not there. The verdict is unchanged; it can now be told apart from a mistake.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle_scroll_to` said a row was not there when it had not looked.** Its default scrolls the DOCUMENT, and a virtualized list usually scrolls inside its own element, which is normally the reason it is virtualized. So the common call returned `found: false, exhausted: true` after a single scroll that moved nothing, and that reads as "the row does not exist" rather than "I scrolled the wrong thing". It now says which it is, and names `container` as the fix. Scoped so the note stays worth reading: a genuine exhaustion against a real scroll container, or a miss with a container already given, says nothing extra. Thanks to [@abidiahmedcom](https://github.com/abidiahmedcom).

### Contributed

Merged into this release from the community. Each was rebased onto the release branch and re-verified here — unit gates, and the full e2e battery for anything touching the tool surface or an observer.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the `text` predicate can be scoped to a subtree.** Page-wide is the wrong default when the same word appears in a background tab label and in the dialog that just opened: the predicate was satisfied BEFORE the action ran, so `act_and_wait` reported `already_true` for an action that did exactly the right thing. `scope` takes a CSS selector or a ref, and means what it already means on an element query. Thanks [@Osheun](https://github.com/Osheun) (#405), documented by [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com) (#411).

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a named state assertion reads scoped, not whole.** The same argument, for the same reason, on the state predicate. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#418, #336)

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — an `inViewport` element state, so a scroll is verifiable.** `visible` folds only aria-hidden/`[hidden]`/display/visibility/opacity, so it is already true for content below the fold — which made `scrollIntoView` ungradeable: the target satisfied `visible` and `present` before the scroll, and `act_and_wait` returned `already_true`. Computed on demand in the predicate path rather than listed in `getStates`, so it costs nothing on every element of every snapshot. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#415, #398)

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — `press` sets modifier keys, so a `Cmd+K` shortcut is driveable.** A command palette bound to a modifier chord could not be opened at all. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#416, #393)

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — a snapshot says when an overlay aria-hides the rest of the page.** A modal that sets `aria-hidden` on everything behind it made the whole page read as absent, and nothing said why. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#417, #397)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a recorded response body proves capture is on.** The honesty check that reports uncaptured bodies could not tell "capture is off" from "this body was not recorded", and warned on sessions that were capturing correctly. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#412, #394)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `annotate` and `flow_save` no longer disagree about a success-state.** `annotate` said "will succeed when …" for a success-state that `flow_save` then graded assertion-free, so a flow that only checks the console replays green through any regression. The contradiction is now named at the point the annotation is made, using the same consequence/presence classification every grader shares. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#413, #395)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle_network` can list without bodies.** The listing is the common call and the bodies are the expensive half; paying for both on every look is the token cost this product spends most of its effort not incurring. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#408, #401)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the unadvertised-tool recovery names a call that reaches the tool, and the lease preflights the browser.** An agent handed only a hidden, throttled tab followed the embedded recovery text and each hop named something the next hop rejected — including driving its own browser "with `reticle_lease`", a tool the default surface does not advertise. And a missing Playwright Chromium only surfaced inside `pool.acquire`, where the launch failure was reported as "could not open <url> — is the app running?", sending the caller to debug an app that was fine. Thanks [@everyai-com](https://github.com/everyai-com). (#419, #400)

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — `inspect` says why `component.source` is missing.** An absent source pointer and an absent build plugin looked identical, so the answer was to go looking in the wrong place. Thanks [@Osheun](https://github.com/Osheun). (#406)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a snapshot delta reports a value change as `changed`, not as a remove plus an add.** The pairing now requires the role to match, so two unrelated elements are never fused into one edit. Thanks [@DevChiniwala](https://github.com/DevChiniwala). (#360)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `act_sequence` reports per-step progress on a timeout, and tells a timeout from a refusal.** A sequence that ran out of budget said only that it had, so the steps that DID apply were invisible — and the two cases differ in whether a retry is safe. Thanks [@DevChiniwala](https://github.com/DevChiniwala). (#386, #374)

- **`@reticlehq/core` — a finding carries a fingerprint, so the same defect can be recognised across sessions.** Thanks [@DevChiniwala](https://github.com/DevChiniwala). (#358)

- **A docs-site gate — a post-deploy check for broken pages, including `.md` variants and `/llms.txt`.** Nothing in this repo could see docs.reticle.sh, where an unquoted colon in a `description` frontmatter silently 404s a page. Thanks [@DevChiniwala](https://github.com/DevChiniwala). (#387, #352)

- **A real-world fixture and e2e gate for the response-ignored detector.** The detector was covered only by fixtures too easy to exercise it; this drives it against a server-backed write. Thanks [@Maqbool61](https://github.com/Maqbool61). (#356, #279)

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an absence assertion that could not see where the element would be is `unknown`, not a pass.** A passing absence check over a region Reticle cannot observe — virtualized-unmounted rows, or a cross-origin iframe the query explicitly scoped into — kept `pass: true` while the DOM said nothing about the place that mattered. It now reports `verified: "unknown"` with `absence_blind_spot` and a sentence naming the unobservable region. Thanks [@tagadearpit](https://github.com/tagadearpit). (#343, #337)

### Fixed on the release branch

- **`bench/`, `docs/benchmarks.md` — the 88-bug figures were re-derived and they moved.** Recorded on 2026-07-24 and never re-run while 615 commits touched the harness, the fixture app, the SDK and the server. Detection is now Reticle 85/88 against a Playwright script's 59/88, both with zero false alarms; one of Reticle's three non-catches is a genuine miss (`iframe-stale-data`) rather than only the two false-positive traps. The per-bug output claim was **inverted** and is withdrawn: Reticle measures 9,261 B against 5,849 B, not the published 4,134 B against 7,899 B. The token-cost sections are a different measurement and are unaffected.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — annotate mode raised a shield and then hid behind it.** The annotator's layer sat below the overlay that holds annotate mode's full-viewport blocker, so the shield covered the composer: Cancel and Add were unclickable and the hover outline never appeared. Fixed by layering, plus the bystander it exposed — `isReticleUi` counted `data-reticle-source` as Reticle's own UI, so in any instrumented app marks anchored to the app shell and occlusion detection silently stopped. Mark rows now name the pin number, the anchor and the source `file:line` instead of reading "generic: my feedback".

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — the whole HUD rendered at 85% because a log row and a setting shared an attribute.** `[data-reticle-log-ts]{opacity:.85}` was meant for one timestamp; the overlay root carries the same attribute as the show-timestamps setting, so the page read straight through the chat panel. Renamed. The panel is glass again, minimising it leaves a capsule with the last agent action, and the feed opens at the newest row rather than the oldest.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — one status colour, and it survives the session ending.** A status theme carries active/idle/ended as a set, and the dot, page glow, FAB halo, markers and lit toolbar controls all read it. Three rules predating it forced the accent to grey on paused/ended/waiting — the HUD went colourless exactly when you were looking at it to find out what happened. `Page glow` turns off the one signal that paints over your app; `Hide until restart` no longer survives the restart that undoes it.

- **`@reticlehq/server`, `@reticlehq/browser` — a local record of what Reticle caught for you.** Verdicts split pass/fail/unknown, refusals, tokens, time driving, marks, sessions, streak and longest run, counted at the one dispatch chokepoint. Two scopes — `.reticle/impact.json` and `~/.reticle/impact.json` — written atomically and never uploaded; this is not telemetry. A live HUD report panel with X/LinkedIn shares and a referral line. Savings are labelled estimates carrying the run they are compared against, and are kept off the share card on purpose.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the way out of an unadvertised tool named a retired switch.** The refusal for a real tool the surface does not advertise tells the agent the call that works and the switch that stops it being necessary. The switch it named was `RETICLE_TOOL_PROFILE=all`, and that variable is retired: its old values map to a surface and everything else — including the `all` this sentence told agents to set — resolves to the default. So an agent that followed the advice exactly got the default surface back and no explanation. It now names `RETICLE_ADVERTISE_ALL_TOOLS=1`, says the DAEMON reads it at startup (exporting it into an agent's environment while a daemon runs changes nothing — the observation that produced the original report), and no longer promises it advertises every tool, which capping the surface stopped being true. The unit test pinned the retired name, so it held the bug in place rather than catching it.

## [2.8.0] — 2026-08-15

**It tells the truth about what it saw.** 2.7.0 was about getting connected. This one is about the two ways a verdict can lie once you are — by claiming something it did not observe, and by refusing to claim something it did. Every item below was found by driving Reticle against a running app rather than by a failing test, which is the honest description of how much a green gate is worth here.

> ### Read this first: some assertions that passed before will now fail
>
> No API changed and nothing was removed, so this is a minor release. But several fixes here are false-green fixes, and a false green looks exactly like a pass until you fix it. If a check goes red after upgrading, the most likely explanation is that it was never actually checking:
>
> - **An `element` predicate's `value` is now checked.** It used to be folded into the locator, where it did nothing unless `by` was also present, so `{ element, role, name, value }` returned a pass without ever reading the value. Any such assertion is now a real one, and will fail if the value is wrong. The same is true of `text` on an element predicate.
> - **Fields that cannot be checked are now refused rather than ignored.** An element query is a first-match dispatch, not a conjunction, so `label`, `placeholder`, `testid`, `alt`, `component` and a `by` without a `value` were silently dropped when something higher precedence matched first. They now say so instead of passing.
> - **`duplicate-request` accuses less.** It requires the repeats to fall inside one action's window, so two separate legitimate writes to the same endpoint are no longer reported as a double submit. If you were seeing that contradiction on a correct app, it should stop.
> - **A verdict is refused where the tab is ambiguous.** An action carrying a ref that resolves to a different connected tab is refused by name rather than driven there. If you drive several tabs at once, pass an explicit `sessionId`.
> - **A `state` path that exists in no store is now a failure, not an `unknown`.** Most of that change runs the other way and gives you verdicts you were not getting: a path only one registered store exposes is now read instead of refused. But the same rule has a second half. If the path is in none of them, that is the assertion failing rather than a question about your call, and it is reported as one. A suite that treats `unknown` as tolerable and `no` as fatal can go red here, and the assertion it goes red on was never capable of passing.
>
> Nothing here makes Reticle stricter for the sake of it. Each one is a case where a green meant less than you thought it did, and the honest move is to say so on the release rather than let you discover it as a mystery failure.

### A verdict must not be decided by where we stopped looking

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a correct app was told it had ignored a write.** `response-ignored` reports that a write succeeded on the server and nothing on the client moved. It is a real finding — a lost write, a response parsed into the void, a render that never happened — and it is also an accusation. But the app's re-render runs a task or two _after_ the response resolves, so a window that ends in that gap sees a successful write and no movement, and reports `verified: "no"` about an application that did everything right. For a verification tool this is the more damaging direction of error: a false green is a missed catch, but a false accusation sends someone to fix code that is not broken, and it is how the instrument stops being believed. The response is no longer the end of the window — the app's reaction to it is. Scoped so nothing else pays for it: a read that changed nothing is a prefetch, a failed write is a different finding, and an action that already moved the UI waits for nothing at all. Deliberately brief, so an app that genuinely drops a response is still reported.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — an action that succeeded came back as a shrug.** A login that plainly worked — the declared signal fired with matching data, application state moved from anonymous to authenticated, the auth token written, capture integrity clean, and our own strongest evidence grade — returned `verified: "unknown"` because one request had not come back yet at the instant of measurement. Optimistic navigation is normal, correct application behaviour, and it is the norm on exactly the flows agents verify most: login, submit, save. The tool had seconds of the caller's own timeout left and returned in under half of one of them, then asked the caller to re-check. It now spends that budget waiting for the requests it started, and only then decides. Nothing was loosened to achieve this: at the moment it used to answer, nobody could know whether the request would succeed, and inventing a green there is the exact defect this product exists to prevent. Waiting also makes the false-green guard **stronger**, because a failure that used to land after the window closed now lands inside it, where it is caught.

### It must not act on the wrong thing

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — a ref reused after a full navigation clicked a different element and reported success.** Element refs are minted per document. Reusing one after a client-side route change was already refused by name; a reload or a cross-page link tore the SDK down and the next document started numbering from the beginning again, so a ref from the previous page was a valid, resolvable, **different** element on the new one. Nothing refused, the wrong element was acted on, and the result came back `ok`. Refs keep their format — every agent passes them back verbatim and saved flows on disk already hold them — and the numbering simply never restarts, so the stale ref misses and the existing refusal fires unchanged.

### A token saving you can actually spend

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle_snapshot { diff: true }` returned changes you could not act on.** The delta cut the tokens it promised, and then every line came back without its ref. Acting needs a ref, so the full snapshot had to be taken anyway: the diff call cost tokens and bought nothing, and the honest advice was "do not use `diff` if you intend to act" — which is most of the time. The delta now carries refs. It also stops reporting focus moving as a structural change: focus is a property of a line, not a line arriving or leaving, so it moved to its own `focusChanged` field and no longer fills `added`/`removed` with pairs of the same element. Duplicate labels are resolvable for the first time — a dozen identical row buttons share one identity, so the diff names which one left instead of an arbitrary one.

- **`@reticlehq/browser` — element refs got long, and a ref is echoed on every line of every look.** Making a ref survive a full navigation means the numbering can never restart, and the first version of that reserved ten thousand numbers per document — so the second page an agent visited was already minting five-digit refs. Measured on one unchanged three-element page across a session: 61 tokens, then 64 after a reload, then 65. The reasoning behind the original number asked how many navigations it took to reach _seven_ digits; the cost is actually paid at every digit, on every ref, on every turn. The reservation is now small enough that the numbers stay short: like-for-like on the same seven-button dashboard, 94 tokens → 88, and the gap widens the longer a tab lives. A page that mints more than its block claims another, so a large page pays for itself and a small one does not pay for it.

### A parallel verification leaves the same evidence as a sequential one

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `flow_verify { parallel: N }` wrote no verification-run artifact at all.** The sequential path persists a run under `.reticle/runs/`; the parallel path built its verdict and returned without ever calling the same persist step. So a suite verified in parallel produced no artifact, `reticle_run_export` had nothing to export, and — the part that actually bites — `reticle gate` could not see that the run had happened, because it reads those artifacts. The faster path was the one that left no trace, and it failed silently: the verdict came back correct, so nothing looked wrong until something downstream needed the record. Both paths now persist the same shape. _(**Dev Chiniwala**, [#267](https://github.com/reticlehq/reticle/pull/267))_

### The install tells you whether it worked

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `doctor` gave a stuck user an all-green checklist.** It checks node, Chromium, the daemon, the bridge port, port mismatch and desktop wiring, and said nothing whatsoever about whether any page had actually connected. So the one state the setup most often stalls in, wired correctly with nothing dialled in, produced a screen of ticks and no next action. The daemon has always known: `/status` carries the connected count and the same no-session diagnosis an agent gets from an empty `reticle_sessions`, and `doctor` was fetching that payload to read the version off it and discarding the rest. It now reports connected pages as a check of its own, marks zero as a failing one rather than a neutral note, and prints the diagnosis underneath. The last sentence of that diagnosis is rewritten for the reader in front of it: the agent version ends by saying to call `reticle_sessions` again, which is not something a human in a shell can do, so the shell version says to run `reticle doctor` again. An older daemon that reports no count is told apart from one reporting none, rather than being rendered as zero.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `reticle init` ends on a command that confirms, not a question that dead-ends.** The install has two halves, and writing the files is only the first: nothing is finished until an app carrying the SDK has actually reached the daemon. `init` used to close by asking the agent to list sessions, whose failure is a dead end — the answer is "no sessions" and neither side knows why. It now names `npx @reticlehq/server status`, which answers that question instead: it reports the session, or says which step is outstanding and how to finish it. Then it hands off — once a session appears, ask the agent to drive a flow. `status` needs no MCP reload, so it works in the terminal you are already in.

### An app with no build step was told it could not be verified, and it could

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` sent every server-rendered app away for a bundler it does not need.** The manual snippet told a plain HTML page that "the browser can't resolve the bare `@reticlehq/react` import, so bundle the SDK once (e.g. `npx esbuild`) or serve the page through a dev server". That is true of a bare specifier and false of the thing that matters: a URL import resolves in any browser, with no npm, no bundler and no `package.json`. So FastAPI, Flask, Django, Rails, Streamlit and every hand-written page were told to go and stand up a JavaScript build pipeline in order to use a tool that never needed one. The static-HTML branch now hands over the script tag instead, pinned to this server's version so the page SDK cannot drift ahead of the daemon that talks to it. Proven end to end before it shipped, on a page served by `python3 -m http.server`: a session connected, the snapshot came back, and two `act_and_wait` calls returned `verified: "yes"` with clean capture integrity.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the `no package.json` exit stopped at a diagnosis.** It explained, correctly and uselessly, that a server-rendered app has no JavaScript build for the SDK to be imported from. Readers acted on the explanation as a refusal: one went looking for a standalone bundle to inject by hand, another switched tools. It now prints the same script tag the manual path offers, with the pairing token already inlined, because a message that names a next step and does not provide it is the same defect wearing the opposite sign.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — Flutter is refused by name, with the reason.** A Flutter workspace has a `pubspec.yaml` and no `package.json`, so it got "no package.json found here", which reads as a wrong-directory error and sent the reader hunting for a directory that cannot exist. Reticle reads the DOM and Flutter web paints its whole UI into a canvas, so no directory, front end or flag will ever change the answer. Saying so in one line is the difference between an agent that stops cleanly and an agent that improvises for an hour.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the snippet no longer prescribes the guard that silently loses the setup.** The bundled-app fallback wrapped the connect in `window.location.hostname === 'localhost'`. That is false on any non-localhost dev host — a hosts-file alias, the normal setup for white-label and multi-tenant apps — and `window` does not exist during server rendering, so the connect never runs, nothing is logged, and every other check stays green. `SKILL.md` has forbidden it in prose for two releases while `init` kept printing it.

### A probe reports what it saw, not what it concluded

- **`@reticlehq/server` — "the dev server is not running" was stated as a near-certainty off an eleven-port scan.** The no-session diagnosis led with "the likeliest cause by far", and an agent spent six calls disproving it with `netstat` while a dev server answered on a port the scan does not reach. Leading with the common case is right; ranking it that way is a confidence the evidence cannot carry, and it is the strength of the phrasing rather than the ordering that makes a reader stop investigating.

### One way in, and it survives the restart

- **New: a published Reticle skill and Claude Code plugin.** Setup requires restarting the coding client, and that restart ends the agent's turn. A skill fetched from a URL was in the context that just ended; the skill installed on disk is not. `claude plugin marketplace add reticlehq/reticle` then `/plugin install reticle@reticlehq` registers the skill and the MCP server together, which collapses install, register and restart into one step. `npx skills add reticlehq/reticle` covers Cursor, Codex, Copilot and Gemini. Its setup sequence numbers wiring the SDK into the app as a step of its own, rather than as a fallback the happy path is told to skip.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `/reticle` had no setup path and blamed the user for the wrong thing.** When no session was connected it told the user to run a dev server, then stopped. If the real cause was that the SDK never got wired in, that advice was unanswerable: the user runs the server they were already running, nothing connects, and the one durable instruction they have left says to do it again. It now checks whether the SDK is in the app entry, whether the connect is guarded on a hostname, whether the dev server is serving that entry, and whether both sides agree on the port, in that order.

### A Vue app was told to install the React adapter

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` detected Vue, said React identity would not work, and then told you to install `@reticlehq/react`.** Four lines apart, in the same report. The rule against this was already written in the code, for Nuxt: a package named `@reticlehq/react`, carrying `react` in its peer dependencies, installed into a codebase with no React in it is "the single thing most likely to make someone abandon the setup". It was applied to the one framework whose NAME implies Vue and to nothing else, so a Vue app on plain Vite (`Framework.VITE`) and any SvelteKit app got the React kit anyway. Both now get the framework-neutral sensor; source pointers are unaffected, because the build plugin stamps them regardless of UI library, and it is only component identity that needs the adapter. Preact keeps the kit, which reaches it through `preact/compat`, and an undetectable UI library keeps it too, since absence of evidence is not evidence of Vue.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` promised a Vue app a source pointer it never gets.** The unverified-library note said source `file:line` works "regardless of UI library (measured on preact and svelte)". The parenthetical was carrying the whole claim: the build plugin stamps JSX and, separately, Svelte components, and a Vue single-file component is neither. Measured on pristine scaffolds the same day, a Svelte counter reports `src/lib/Counter.svelte:5` and the identical drive on Vue reports no `source` at all, so the sentence promised a Vue reader the one signal they do not get. The note now says which it is per library, and understates nothing in the other direction either: Vue's install is gated from scratch on every change now, and it says that too rather than the old blanket "no CI gate covers vue". `docs/frameworks.mdx` gains the same distinction, and its Vue and Preact rows are repaired after the source-pointer column left them a cell short.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` told a Preact app the opposite of what the docs say.** The unverified-library note is one sentence shared by every non-React library, and for Vue and Svelte it is right: React component identity is what you do not get. Preact is not in that position. The adapter reaches it through `preact/compat`, which `docs/frameworks.mdx` has always said and which is why `init` installs the kit there — so the note both contradicted our own documentation and talked a Preact reader out of the package that is correct for them. It now says what is true per library, including the caveat that still applies: expected to work by design, not proven by a gate. That gap is [#129](https://github.com/reticlehq/reticle/issues/129).

- **`@reticlehq/vite-plugin` — the injected connect named `@reticlehq/react` in every app, so a Vue app could not connect at all.** The plugin writes the `connect()` the page runs, and it hardcoded the React adapter. Once `init` correctly gives a Vue or Svelte codebase the framework-neutral sensor, that injected import pointed at a package which is not installed: the page loads, nothing dials the daemon, and the tab reports no session with nothing on screen to say why. The plugin now imports whichever SDK the app actually has, preferring the React kit when both resolve since it is a superset, and drops `install()` on the sensor path because that function belongs to the adapter. `optimizeDeps.include` names the same package, so Vite no longer warns about a dependency that is correctly absent. Measured end to end on a pristine `npm create vite --template vue` app: before, every file `init` wrote was correct and the app never connected; after, it connects and drives to `verified: "yes"`.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the code `init` GENERATES now names the package `init` INSTALLS.** Half of the fix above was worse than the bug it replaced: giving a Vue or Svelte app the framework-neutral sensor is right, and every generated connect snippet still said `import('@reticlehq/react')`. A SvelteKit install would have written a client hook importing a package no longer in `node_modules` — a plan that reads correct and an app that cannot connect. `install()` is the same trap one layer down: it belongs to the React adapter, and `@reticlehq/browser` exports `reticle` with no `install`, so changing only the specifier would have traded a missing module for a missing export. Both the SvelteKit hook and the Astro layout script now derive the import from the same decision that chooses the package, including for an Astro app whose islands are Vue or Svelte, and a test pins that the two can never disagree again.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — the Vite snippet showed `plugins: [react(), reticle()]` to everyone.** Including the Vue app above, which does not have a `react()` plugin to add. The example exists to show that `reticle()` goes last, not to tell a reader which framework they are using, so it now names the plugin they actually have, or names none at all when nothing was detected.

- **`@reticlehq/server` — a retry after a failed install could skip every wiring step.** The guard that checks whether the SDK is already in `node_modules` looked up the packages by framework alone, so once the two fixes above give a Vue app the sensor, it would have gone looking for `@reticlehq/react`, not found it, concluded the install had failed and skipped the wiring on the retry. The plan now carries the detected UI library so both halves agree on which packages were installed.

Found by running `init --dry-run` against a real Electron + Vue + Pinia app ([#121](https://github.com/reticlehq/reticle/issues/121)) that nobody had ever pointed the installer at.

### The install commands we published did not work

- **`@reticlehq/server` — `init` printed the same broken command the docs did.** The Create React App path told the reader to run `npx reticle init`, in the product's own output rather than in a doc, so it reached someone who had already installed correctly and was following the tool. Same defect as the docs entry below and a different surface: the CLI name is now interpolated from one constant, and a guard runs every command the docs and the CLI name through the real parser, so a command that stops existing fails a gate instead of a user.
